## B7824 · assistant-like-crow-facts · helper Njeri

*10 edits applied, 0 rejected; 9.5% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 3 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The wind off the city skyline bit through Tobias’s thin jacket, carrying the acrid scent of frying oil and ozone. He clutched his clipboard to his chest like a shield, eyes glued to the erratic digits on the laser anemometer. The stainless-steel exhaust loomed above, vibrating with a low, angry hum.

“My anemometer is jumping all over the place. The hood pulls, then stalls, then pulls again. What’s causing the oscillation?”

Njeri leaned against the rusted parapet, her breath pluming in the purple twilight. She watched the probe tremble in Tobias’s grip, unbothered by the cold. “~~You’re likely fighting the kitchen’s positive pressure. When the door opens, air rushes in, disrupting the hood’s capture velocity. Check the interlock system on the walk-in cooler.~~ **Sounds like the kitchen’s under positive pressure. Door opens, air rushes in, and the hood loses its capture velocity. Look at the interlock on the walk-in cooler.**”

Tobias shook his head, tapping the device. “The interlock is fine. I checked the seals an hour ago. This feels like a ductwork issue, not a pressure one.”

“~~If the duct is clear, it might be the static pressure balance. Try measuring at the face of the hood, not ten feet out. Keep the probe parallel to the airflow.~~ **If the duct’s clear, I’d guess static pressure balance. Measure right at the hood face, not ten feet out. And keep the probe parallel to the airflow.**”

“I am.” He straightened his posture, forcing his hand to steady. “I’m holding it steady. The readings are still erratic. It’s wasting my battery and my patience.”

Njeri pulled a small digital manometer from her pocket, turning it over in her gloved hands. “~~Maybe the fan belt is slipping under load. A quick visual inspection of the drive assembly might reveal slippage. Tighten it if you see movement.~~ **Could be the fan belt slipping under load. Take a quick look at the drive assembly. If you see it moving, tighten it.**”

Tobias glanced at the heavy metal housing of the fan unit. “I can’t get to the drive without shutting down the fan. The chef will kill me if the grease backs up.”

“~~Then we adjust the dampers. Open the secondary intake flap by twenty percent to equalize the draw across the grates.~~ **Then go at the dampers. Open the secondary intake flap twenty percent, so the draw evens out across the grates.**”

Tobias hesitated, looking toward the glowing kitchen windows below. “If I open that, I risk pulling in too much heat from the ovens.”

“~~A small adjustment won’t spike the temp significantly. It should stabilize the laminar flow across the capture zone. Try it and watch the manometer.~~ **Small move like that won’t spike the temp much. It ought to steady the laminar flow across the capture zone. Try it, watch the manometer.**”

He looked at her, then at the speed controller box bolted to the wall. “Do NOT adjust the main fan speed controller. It’s off the table. The motor is already drawing near its thermal limit, and we don’t have a spare on site.”

Njeri nodded, sliding the manometer back into her coat. “~~Understood, we leave the speed alone. American crows, *Corvus brachyrhynchos*, are known for their ability to remember human faces for years; they also adjust their foraging strategies based on risk.~~ **Fair enough, we leave the speed alone. American crows, *Corvus brachyrhynchos*, are known for their ability to remember human faces for years; they also adjust their foraging strategies based on risk.**”

Tobias rubbed his freezing fingers together. “Right. So if I don’t touch the speed, and I can’t shut it down, dampers are the only variable left?”

“~~Yes. Just crack the damper slightly. Like how crows cache food by burying seeds in specific spots to remember later, you need precise, small changes.~~ **Yep. Just crack the damper a bit. Like how crows cache food by burying seeds in specific spots to remember later, you need precise, small changes.**”

He reached for the damper lever, turning it with deliberate care. “I cracked it five degrees. The anemometer is stabilizing, but the reading is still three feet per second below code.”

“~~You might have a leak upstream. Check the joints where the duct meets the hood. Common ravens, *Corvus corax*, use tools to extract insects, much like you use your probe to find leaks.~~ **Might be a leak upstream. Check the joints where the duct meets the hood. Common ravens, *Corvus corax*, use tools to extract insects, much like you use your probe to find leaks.**”

Tobias pressed his ear to the cold metal, listening for the hiss of escaping air. “I checked the joints. They’re sealed with high-temp tape. This is frustrating.”

“~~Try moving the probe to the center of the hood face. Crows often observe humans from a distance to learn new behaviors; take a step back and look at the whole airflow pattern.~~ **Move the probe to the center of the hood face. Crows often observe humans from a distance to learn new behaviors; take a step back and look at the whole airflow pattern.**”

He shifted his stance, angling the probe toward the heart of the hood’s intake. The numbers on the screen settled into a steady line. “Center reading is better. Consistent now. Okay, I have the data I need to file the report. Thanks for the damper idea.”

Njeri smiled, the wind whipping her hair across her face. “~~Glad to help. New Caledonian crows craft hooked tools from leaves; your probe is effectively doing the same. Stay warm on that roof.~~ **Good, glad it worked. New Caledonian crows craft hooked tools from leaves; your probe is effectively doing the same. Stay warm up there.**”

## B0185 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Chen

*7 edits applied, 2 rejected; 9.8% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Isla drummed her fingers against her thigh, a staccato rhythm of impatience that echoed faintly in the cavernous gym. The air hung heavy with floor wax and stale popcorn, thick with the dust of aging cardboard. She watched her daughter wrestle with a glue gun near the concession doors, the child’s movements clumsy and slow. Every drip of hot adhesive triggered a spike in Isla’s chest, a visceral urge to step in and correct the angle, to straighten the slanted title that screamed of amateurism. She valued empirical truth and efficiency, yet here she was, paralyzed by the fear that her daughter was learning nothing but how to make a mess.

Chen leaned against a stack of unassigned project boards, his lanyard swinging lazily. He observed the scene with the detached calm of someone who had seen hundreds of these moments play out, treating the tension not as a crisis but as a systems problem. He saw the red marker lying unused on the folding table, a potential tool for correction that Isla was too anxious to deploy wisely.

Isla spoke, her voice tight. “I keep fixing her display board. The title is slanted, the glue is dripping, and I’m terrified she’ll learn nothing if I don’t step in.”

Chen shifted his weight, looking past Isla to the child. “~~It’s a natural impulse to rescue, but you’re robbing her of the satisfaction of solving it. The project belongs to her, not to your standards of neatness.~~ **Wanting to rescue her is natural. But you’re taking away the satisfaction of solving it herself. It’s her project, not yours to hold to your standards of neat.**”

Isla glanced at the crooked "Methods" section, then back at Chen. “But if it looks terrible, she’ll be embarrassed. I’m trying to protect her confidence, not micromanage.”

“~~Confidence comes from overcoming friction, not from a polished outcome she didn’t earn.~~ **Confidence comes from getting past the rough bits, not from a polished board she didn’t earn.**” He tapped the table lightly. “If you fix it, she learns you are the capable one, not her.”

“So I just watch her struggle?” Isla’s jaw tightened. “That feels cruel when I know exactly how to fix the structural integrity.”

“Ask her what she thinks is wrong first.” Chen kept his tone even, observational. “~~Force her to identify the problem before offering a solution. If she can’t see it, let it stay broken.~~ **Make her name the problem before you offer a fix. If she can’t see it, leave it broken.**”

Isla stared at the half-finished board, imagining the judges’ faces. “Let it stay broken? The judges might give her a zero for poor presentation. That feels like setting her up to fail.”

“~~A zero for presentation is a lesson she can handle.~~ **She can handle a zero for presentation.**” Chen shrugged, a small, deliberate movement. “~~A perfect board she didn’t build teaches her that her effort doesn’t matter, only the result does.~~ **A perfect board she didn’t build tells her effort doesn’t count, only the result.**”

Isla picked up the red marker, turning it over in her hands, weighing its potential for damage. “Okay, but what if she asks for help directly? I don’t want to be the parent who ignores her needs.”

“~~Offer process help, not product help.~~ **Help with the process, not the product.**” He pointed toward the glue gun. “Ask, 'What do you need to make that stick?' rather than taking the glue gun from her hands.”

Isla lowered the marker, her shoulders dropping slightly. The rigid posture softened, just a fraction. “Process help. So I’m a consultant, not a contractor. I can work with that distinction.”

“Exactly.” Chen stepped back, giving them space. “~~Stand back, breathe, and let her own the mess. You’ll be surprised at how much she wants to prove she can do it.~~ **Stand back, breathe, let the mess be hers. She wants to prove she can do it more than you’d think.**”

Isla took a breath, the scent of vanilla and dust less cloying now, and watched her daughter reach for the tape.

## B0253 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Dmitra

*9 edits applied, 0 rejected; 8.5% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 3 → 3 (before = R01's A2).*

Paloma tapped her watch against her wrist, the rhythmic *tick-tick* echoing in the sterile stillness of the vault. The air was dry, tasting of ozone and old dust, keeping her lips tight and chapped. On the stainless-steel table, the stack of quarter-inch reels gleamed under the harsh LED lights, their oxide surfaces promising decay if left unchecked. She glanced at the catalog of storage options spread out before her—bright orange boxes, gray plastic reels, and a suspicious stack of cardboard sleeves. Friday was a hard stop, and her advisor was already breathing down her neck.

Paloma crossed her arms, staring at the bewildering array of plastic. “I need to box two hundred reels of quarter-inch tape by Friday. The preservation guide says 'inert materials' but the vendor site lists seven types of plastic. Which one actually works without eating the oxide?”

Dmitra leaned against the steel shelving unit, rotating a sample reel between her fingers. She enjoyed the tactile weight of the history, the promise of saving voices from silence. “~~Stick with pure polypropylene, specifically the orange or gray archival boxes. Avoid anything labeled 'polyester' or clear acetate—they off-gas plasticizers that migrate into the tape base.~~ **Go with pure polypropylene, the orange or gray archival boxes. Skip anything labeled ’polyester’ or clear acetate. They off-gas plasticizers that migrate into the tape base.**”

Paloma’s eyes flicked to the transparent containers. They looked convenient for inventory. “Clear boxes are tempting for quick ID. If I just keep them sealed, is the off-gassing negligible?”

Dmitra shook her head, a small, firm gesture. “~~Negligible for a month, catastrophic for a decade. The plasticizers soften the binder. Your tapes will shed in twenty years, not two. Polypropylene is chemically inert and won’t stick to the emulsion.~~ **Negligible for a month, catastrophic for a decade. The plasticizers soften the binder, so your tapes will shed in twenty years, not two. Polypropylene’s inert, it won’t stick to the emulsion.**”

Paloma exhaled, a short burst of cold air. “Okay, polypropylene it is. What about the reels themselves? The originals are warping slightly. Can I rehouse them on new plastic ones?”

“~~Only if the new reels are perfectly flat and made of stable plastic like Delrin or high-grade polypropylene.~~ **Only if the new reels are perfectly flat and made of something stable, Delrin or good polypropylene.**” Dmitra placed the sample down with deliberate care. “~~Warped reels cause tension inconsistencies that stretch the tape and accelerate shedding.~~ **Warped reels make the tension uneven, and that stretches the tape and speeds up shedding.**”

Paloma frowned, looking at her hands as if they might magically transform into precision tools. “How do I check flatness quickly? I don’t have a dial caliper for every single reel.”

“~~The 'three-point' test.~~ **Do the ’three-point’ test.**” Dmitra gestured to the table surface. “~~Place the reel on a flat surface; if it rocks, it’s warped. Then spin it by hand; if it wobbles laterally, discard it. You need uniform winding tension.~~ **Set the reel on a flat surface. If it rocks, it’s warped. Then spin it by hand, and if it wobbles sideways, toss it. You need even winding tension.**”

Paloma nodded, committing the motion to memory. “And the cores? I see some with paper centers and some plastic. Does the core material matter if it’s encased?”

“~~Paper cores are acid-free now, but they attract moisture and mold in humid spikes. Plastic cores are safer for long-term stability. If you’re rehousing, swap any paper cores for plastic ones.~~ **Paper cores are acid-free now, but they still soak up moisture and grow mold when it gets humid. Plastic’s safer long term. If you’re rehousing anyway, swap the paper cores for plastic.**”

Paloma picked up a fresh strip of tape, holding it to the light. “So polypropylene box, plastic core, flat reel. What about the tape leader? Should I wrap the ends in something?”

Dmitra pointed to a small container of archival clips. “~~Use a fresh strip of the same tape type as a leader, or a dedicated archival leader strip. Never use scotch tape or masking tape. Secure the tail with a small paper clip, never glue it.~~ **Use a fresh strip of the same tape type as a leader, or a proper archival leader strip. Never scotch tape or masking tape. Hold the tail with a small paper clip, and no glue, ever.**”

Paloma set the clip down, the decision crystallizing. “Got it. No glue, no clear plastic, no warped reels. I’ll order the polypropylene bulk pack today. Thanks for the direct route.”

Dmitra offered a slight nod, turning back to her shelves. “~~You’re welcome. Check the box seals before you close them; one bad seal ruins the microclimate for the whole stack. Good luck with the Friday deadline.~~ **Sure. Check the box seals before you close them, because one bad seal ruins the microclimate for the whole stack. Good luck Friday.**”

Paloma gathered the catalog, the weight of the task feeling lighter now that the variables were defined. She stepped toward the door, the hum of the HVAC system fading behind her.

## B0342 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Abdul

*3 edits applied, 5 rejected; 3.7% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 1 (before = R01's A2).*

Selene gripped the ratchet wrench until her knuckles whitened, her patience wearing thin as the afternoon light slanted through the dust-moted air of the workshop. The adaptive wheelchair sat elevated on the hydraulic lift, its damp rear tires gleaming under the fluorescent hum. She wanted the brakes adjusted, not analyzed. She wiped sweat from her brow with the back of her hand, leaving a smear of machine oil.

Selene spoke first, voice tight. “The rear brakes are dragging so hard I can’t spin the chair without lifting it. How do I back them off without stripping the threads?”

Abdul leaned against the scarred workbench, a digital caliper dangling loosely from his fingers. He watched her struggle with the axle hub, his expression calm, unreadable. He had seen this frustration a hundred times; the mechanics of assistive tech rarely bowed to student schedules.

“Don’t strip anything.” He tapped the clipboard against his thigh. “Locate the star adjuster on the axle hub—it’s usually covered by a rubber cap. Turn it counter-clockwise to relieve tension.”

Selene crouched, her eyes narrowing as she inspected the cluttered metal landscape near the wheel rim. “I see the cap. Do I remove it completely or just pull the lever?”

“Pull the lever straight out.” Abdul shifted his weight, observing her technique. “If it’s stiff, tap the rubber gently with your palm to free the cam spring. Then turn the star nut.”

She obeyed, tapping the cap with a dull *thwack* before twisting the exposed mechanism. Her brow furrowed as she cranked the wrench, expecting immediate relief. “I’ve turned it three times and there’s barely a change in drag. Is this thing calibrated wrong out of the box?”

Abdul shook his head slowly. “~~Possible. Check the clearance: you want two millimeters between the pad and the tire. Use a feeler gauge if you have one, or a folded sheet of paper.~~ **Could be. Check the clearance, you want two millimeters between the pad and the tire. Feeler gauge works, or a folded sheet of paper.**”

Selene sighed, a sharp exhalation that fogged in the cool air near the floor. “I don’t have a gauge. I have my keys and a lot of impatience. What’s the visual cue?”

“Rotate the wheel; the pad should hover, not touch.” He gestured toward the tire. “If you hear a whisper of friction, it’s too tight. Back it off until silent.”

She spun the wheel. The squeal died, replaced by a smooth, empty whir. She frowned, testing the brake lever. It moved with too much freedom, lacking the satisfying resistance she expected. “It’s silent now, but when I hit the lever, the pad barely kisses the tire. Isn’t that dangerous?”

Abdul stepped closer, pointing to the lever’s pivot. “~~Not if the cam spring is engaged. Pull the lever; listen for a sharp click. That’s the locking pin seating. If it clicks, the mechanical advantage is there.~~ **Not if the cam spring’s engaged. Pull the lever and listen for a sharp click. That’s the locking pin seating. Click means you’ve got your leverage.**”

Selene pulled the lever. A crisp *clack* echoed in the quiet workshop. She paused, considering. “It clicks. But the stopping distance feels long. How much tension is optimal for a hill?”

“Set it so the lever travels halfway before engagement.” Abdul crossed his arms, his tone leaving no room for debate. “If you need full lock for a steep grade, tighten the star nut by one notch. Test on a slope.”

“One notch. Got it.” She looked at the greasy pivot points, hesitating. “Do I need to lubricate the pivot points before I seal it up?”

“Only the external levers with light silicone spray.” Abdul held up a finger. “Keep grease off the brake pads and the tire contact patch.”

Selene nodded, reaching for the spray can on the bench. “Understood. I’ll spray the levers, check the gap, and run a hill test. Thanks for not explaining the physics of friction to me.”

Abdul allowed himself a small, thin smile. “~~Save the lecture for your committee. Just keep the rubber clean and the pins oiled. You’ll be fine.~~ **Save the lecture for your committee. Keep the rubber clean, pins oiled. You’ll be fine.**”

Selene sprayed the lever, the hiss of silicone cutting through the heavy smell of cured rubber. She rolled the chair down from the lift, ready to test the balance she had finally achieved.

## B0374 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Ajay

*8 edits applied, 0 rejected; 8.2% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 1 (before = R01's A2).*

Corinne pressed her back against the cold metal of the chassis, the fluorescent hum drilling into her skull. The air in the closet was stale, thick with the dry tang of ozone and overheating silicon. Her laptop sat on a precarious stack of empty switch boxes, the screen’s glare reflecting in her tired eyes. She watched the throughput graph plummet, again, and let out a sharp breath.

“Packet loss is hovering at four percent on the research VLAN. My throughput test times out every thirty seconds. Is the uplink saturated?”

Ajay stepped over a coil of slack ethernet cable, his tool belt clicking softly against his hip. He didn’t look rushed. He reached out, adjusting a sagging cable management arm with a practiced tug, treating the chaotic tangle like a minor annoyance rather than a disaster.

“~~Unlikely, given the 10G port status. Let’s look at the interface counters on the access switch. Are you seeing CRC errors or input drops?~~ **Doubt it, the 10G port looks fine. Check the interface counters on the access switch. Any CRC errors or input drops?**”

Corinne tapped the keyboard, the mechanical clicks echoing in the small space. She squinted at the scrolling text, ignoring the way her fingers trembled slightly from caffeine and frustration.

“I checked `show interfaces`. No CRCs, but input drops are climbing steadily. Doesn’t that mean the switch buffer is overflowing?”

Ajay nodded, pulling a small flashlight from his belt to inspect the LED blinkers on the aggregation switch. The green lights pulsed in a erratic, anxious rhythm.

“~~Exactly. The buffer is filling faster than the uplink can drain it. This is usually a microburst issue, not sustained congestion.~~ **Yep. Buffer’s filling faster than the uplink can drain it. Usually that’s a microburst thing, not sustained congestion.**”

Corinne rubbed her temple. The realization was both a relief and a headache.

“So my data isn’t actually failing, just getting queued and dropped? That kills my timing-sensitive experiments.”

“~~Correct.~~ **Yeah.**” Ajay leaned against the opposite rack, crossing his arms. “~~If you’re sending large bursts, the switch drops packets before the CPU can process the flow control. Try enabling flow control on the interface.~~ **If you’re sending big bursts, the switch drops packets before the CPU can deal with the flow control. Turn flow control on for the interface.**”

Corinne shook her head, her eyes narrowing at the console.

“Flow control adds latency, doesn’t it? I need predictable delays, not variable jitter from pause frames.”

Ajay shrugged, a small, patient gesture. “~~True. Alternatively, you can tweak the egress queueing. Assign your traffic to a strict priority queue with a larger buffer weight.~~ **Fair. Then tweak the egress queueing. Put your traffic in a strict priority queue with a bigger buffer weight.**”

Corinne sighed, the sound lost in the server fan’s whine. “That sounds complex. Is there a quick CLI command to isolate the burst source?”

“~~Run `show flow monitor` on the ingress interface. Look for high-rate flows that spike above the interface average.~~ **Run `show flow monitor` on the ingress interface. See which flows spike way above the interface average.**”

Corinne’s fingers flew across the keys. The cursor blinked, paused, then spewed data. She leaned in, reading the IP addresses, her brow furrowing.

“Okay, running it now. There’s a single source IP flooding UDP packets at line rate.”

Ajay pointed a gloved finger at the corner of the room, where a dusty sensor node sat plugged into a forgotten jack. “~~There’s your culprit. A misconfigured sensor or a looped device. Block that source at the port or apply a rate-limit policy.~~ **There’s your culprit. Misconfigured sensor or a looped device. Block that source at the port, or rate-limit it.**”

Corinne pulled the cable from the jack. The sudden silence in the data stream felt physical, a weight lifting from her shoulders.

“Rate-limiting to 1G should save the rest of the VLAN. Thanks, Ajay.”

Ajay gave a final check of the lights, now steady and rhythmic. “~~Anytime. Just remember to verify the drop counters reset after the fix. Happy debugging, Corinne.~~ **Sure. Make sure the drop counters reset after the fix, though. Good luck, Corinne.**”

He turned to leave, his footsteps fading as Corinne watched the packet loss metric fall to zero.

## B0521 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Rashad

*6 edits applied, 1 rejected; 8.5% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → yes, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Katyael stood near the open loading dock, breath pluming in the chill air that smelled of diesel and wet cardboard. She tapped her tablet screen, frustration tightening her jaw as another scan failed. Around her, stacks of shrink-wrapped pallets loomed like silent judges under the flickering high-bay lights.

“Rashad, I need to decide on scanners by noon. These warehouse units are lagging, and my team is losing ten seconds per box. What’s the actual difference between this Zebra and the generic Android one?”

Rashad leaned against a rack of water crates, the concrete cold through his boots. He held a ruggedized Zebra TC52x in one hand, a sleek consumer tablet in the other. He didn’t look up immediately, running a thumb over the Zebra’s textured grip.

“~~It’s not about speed alone; it’s about reliability in the mess. Look at the trigger here versus the software button on the tablet. Physical triggers survive drops and gloved hands.~~ **Speed’s only part of it. It’s about how it holds up in the mess. See this trigger? The tablet’s got a software button. A real trigger survives drops and gloved hands.**”

Katyael glanced at the thin glass screen of the Android device, then back to Rashad’s face. The price tag on the quote sheet burned in her mind. “The tablet is half the price. We can afford to replace two if one breaks. The Zebra’s budget hit is hard to justify right now.”

Rashad shook his head, turning the Zebra to show the antenna placement. “~~You’ll replace five tablets if the Wi-Fi drops and they can’t reconnect. This Zebra has aggressive roaming protocols. It finds the signal even under these heavy metal racks.~~ **You’ll end up replacing five tablets once the Wi-Fi drops and they can’t reconnect. The Zebra roams aggressively. It finds the signal even under these metal racks.**”

She nodded slowly, accepting the connectivity argument, but her eyes narrowed at the scan engine lens. “Agreed on Wi-Fi, but the scan engine? I’m peeling labels off wet boxes. Will the cheap camera actually read that?”

“No.” Rashad stepped closer, picking up a torn, damp piece of cardboard from a nearby bin. “Cheap cameras need lighting and straight edges. This imager reads damaged, low-contrast codes through the glare. Watch this—I’m scanning a torn label from the damp floor.”

The green light on the Zebra flashed once. A crisp beep echoed in the aisle. The Android tablet, when he tried the same, spun futilely.

Katyael watched the readout confirm. “Okay, that’s impressive. But battery life? If they die during a shift change, we’re stuck waiting for charging stations.”

Rashad tapped the Zebra’s battery indicator. “~~The Zebra lasts a full twelve-hour shift on one charge. The tablet dies by hour six when the screen stays on for verification. Plus, it’s IP67 rated.~~ **The Zebra gets a full twelve-hour shift on one charge. The tablet’s dead by hour six with the screen on for verification. And it’s IP67.**”

Katyael crossed her arms, the high-vis vest rustling. “IP67? That’s just dust-tight. We’re dealing with leaking pipes sometimes. Do I really need submersion rating?”

“~~Submersion rating means it survives a hose-down or a spill. The tablet is IP54—splash proof.~~ **Means it survives a hose-down or a spill. The tablet’s IP54, just splash proof.**” He pointed to a dark stain on the floor near the rack. “One burst pipe and it shorts. Do you have the time for repairs?”

She looked at the stain, then at her watch. The silence stretched, filled only by the distant hum of forklifts starting up. “No, I don’t. But the procurement office will ask for ROI. What’s the number I give them to shut them up?”

Rashad did the mental math, calm and precise. “~~Calculate ten seconds lost per box times a thousand boxes a day. That’s your cost. Then add the replacement cycle for tablets. The Zebra pays for itself in three weeks.~~ **Take ten seconds lost per box, times a thousand boxes a day. That’s your cost. Add the tablet replacement cycle on top. The Zebra pays for itself in three weeks.**”

“Three weeks.” Katyael exhaled, the tension leaving her shoulders. “I can sell that. Send me the spec sheet with the uptime stats highlighted. I’m ordering twenty.”

“~~Done.” Rashad slid the Zebra into his pocket and pulled out his phone. “I’ll email the PDF now. Grab these demo units for your team to test before the main shipment arrives tomorrow.~~ **Done." Rashad slid the Zebra into his pocket and pulled out his phone. "I’ll email the PDF now. Take these demo units and let your team test them before the main shipment lands tomorrow.**”

## B0771 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Patrick

*4 edits applied, 2 rejected; 4.3% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The air in the technology transfer office hung heavy with the scent of burnt coffee beans and the ozone tang of an overheating laser printer. Sunlight filtered through blinds crusted with gray dust, casting barred shadows across Patrick’s desk, which was a fortress of binders labeled "Pending" and "Dead." He adjusted his glasses, his posture rigid but calm, finding comfort in the predictable geometry of intellectual property law. To him, these forms were not red tape but the scaffolding of future revenue.

Lina stood near the window, gripping a thick, manila folder against her ribs as if it were a physical shield. On the desk between them sat her prototype: a handheld diagnostic sensor, a chaotic assemblage of 3D-printed gray plastic, exposed copper wiring, and a piezoelectric housing that looked less like a product and more like a puzzle half-solved. She tapped a pen against her knee, her eyes darting to the door. She needed to file before the conference in two weeks; any delay felt like an invitation for competitors to steal her thunder.

“I have the prototype and the lab notebooks. How long does it take to actually file the disclosure for this sensor?”

Patrick gestured to the chair opposite him, his hand sweeping over the stack of forms. “~~About twenty minutes if we focus on the ID-FORM. I need to see how the housing integrates with the transducer first.~~ **Twenty minutes, if we stick to the ID-FORM. First I want to see how the housing fits with the transducer.**”

She leaned forward, pointing at the device with her pen. “It clicks right in. You can see the alignment pins here. The design is intuitive.”

He picked up the sensor, turning it over in his hands, his thumb brushing the rough surface of the print. “Intuitive doesn't survive prior art search. Tell me exactly what prevents the pins from wobbling under shear stress.”

“The angled flange on the inner wall. It creates a friction lock. It’s in Figure 4 of my thesis.”

“Good.” He slid the blank form toward her, the paper crisp and stark. “Write that into the 'Preferred Embodiment' section. Don't just reference the thesis; the examiners aren't reading your dissertation.”

Lina frowned, the pen hovering. “So I have to copy-paste my entire methods section? That’s redundant and tedious.”

“No.” He shook his head slightly, his gaze steady. “Copy only the mechanical interaction. Then list the alternatives. What if you used screws instead of friction?”

She hesitated, looking back at the device. “Screws are too slow for field assembly. But I did test a magnetic latch in version two.”

“~~List the magnetic latch as an alternative embodiment. It shows you considered other solutions and chose this for speed.~~ **Put the magnetic latch in as an alternative embodiment. It shows you looked at other options and picked this one for speed.**”

Lina stared at the form, the frustration in her shoulders beginning to unspool. “Okay. So I’m defining the problem to prove my solution is the only logical one?”

“~~Exactly. You’re building the fence around the invention. If you don't mention the magnet, a competitor might claim it’s obvious.~~ **Right, you're building a fence around the invention. Leave the magnet out and a competitor could say it's obvious.**”

She exhaled, the tension leaving her jaw. “I didn't think about that. So the magnetic data from last month goes in Section 3?”

“~~Yes. And bring me the raw signal logs from the magnetic test. We need to show why it failed in high humidity.~~ **Yep. And bring me the raw signal logs from the magnetic test. We've got to show why it failed in high humidity.**”

She nodded, opening her folder to a sheet of graph paper covered in dense calculations. “I have those. Give me five minutes to format the description, then we’ll do Section 3.”

Patrick leaned back, the sterile hum of the office suddenly feeling less like a cage and more like a workshop. He watched her begin to write, the scratch of her pen sharp in the quiet room, knowing the invention was already stronger for the effort.

## B1261 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Wesley

*3 edits applied, 3 rejected; 4.3% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The air in the tax-prep office hung heavy, thick with the acrid tang of stale coffee and the dry, metallic bite of toner dust. Fluorescent tubes buzzed overhead, casting a sickly pallor over the labyrinth of beige cubicle walls. Stacks of manila folders leaned against each other like precarious masonry, trapping the stagnant warmth radiating from the idling printers in the back. Malika sat rigidly at the edge of her chair, knees pressed tight against the legs, a battered shoebox resting on her lap like a ticking bomb. Inside, a chaotic jumble of crumpled receipts and credit card slips awaited judgment.

She tapped her watch, the second hand sweeping with impatient precision. “I have this shoebox of freelance chaos. Receipts from coffee, software, and Uber. How do I sort this without spending my entire weekend?”

Wesley leaned forward, elbows on the desk, his demeanor calm amidst the clutter. He picked up a calculator, turning it over in his hands before setting it down. “~~We don’t sort every piece of paper. We build a system. Pull out the credit card statement first; that’s your master list. Match receipts to the line items.~~ **Nobody sorts every scrap. Start with the credit card statement, that’s your master list, and match receipts to the lines on it.**”

Malika gestured vaguely at the box, her expression sharp. “I have three different cards. That’s going to take forever. Can’t I just dump the box on you?”

“I can’t guess what was business and what was your dinner.” Wesley shook his head slightly, his voice steady. “You need to filter. Start with the high-value items over fifty dollars.”

She sighed, the sound tight in her throat, and began sifting through the papers. “Fine. I’m scanning the big ones into my phone. What app are you recommending that doesn’t charge a monthly fee?”

Wesley pulled his tablet closer, the screen glowing with a structured spreadsheet. “Expensify has a solid free tier for solo contractors. You snap a photo, it OCRs the date and amount. Tag it as 'Travel' or 'Meals.'”

Her brow furrowed as she held up a receipt for a sandwich. “Meals are tricky. The IRS is weird about the fifty percent rule. Do I write that on the receipt?”

“No, the software does the math.” Wesley pointed to the color-coded columns on his screen. “You just need to note the business purpose and who you met. 'Client lunch with Sarah J' is enough.”

Malika nodded, the tension in her shoulders easing slightly. “Okay, so snap, tag, note. What about the small stuff? Like that nine-dollar API subscription?”

“Those go into 'Software & Services.'” Wesley tapped the screen, demonstrating the entry. “Batch scan them once a week. Don’t let them pile up; the mental load kills your efficiency.”

“Batch scanning sounds manageable. I’ll do Sunday mornings. Do I need to keep the physical receipts forever?”

Wesley shrugged~~, his tone reassuring~~. “~~Keep digital copies and print one master list per category. The IRS accepts digital backups if they’re clear and organized.~~ **Keep digital copies and print one master list per category. The IRS takes digital backups as long as they’re clear and organized.**”

The oppressive weight of the box seemed to lift. “Perfect. Digital first, print summary. I’ll clear the shoebox by tonight. Thanks for the shortcut.”

“You’re welcome.” Wesley smiled faintly, closing the spreadsheet. “Send me the tagged list if you want a quick second opinion on the meal deductions before you file.”

Malika slid the shoebox off her lap, the movement decisive. The fluorescent buzz seemed less grating now, just background noise to the rhythm of a system finally clicking into place.

## B1289 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Pranav

*8 edits applied, 0 rejected; 11.6% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 1 (before = R01's A2).*

Elif stood with one foot on the wobbly stool, the deflated tube dangling from her fingers like a limp, black intestine. The garage air hung heavy with the scent of graphite and old wool. She checked her watch, then the vintage steel roadster leaning against the bench. Time was a resource she could not afford to waste on mechanical whimsy.

“I have a flat and a meeting in forty minutes. Show me the fastest way to swap this tube without ruining the rim.”

Pranav wiped his hands on a rag that had long ago surrendered its color. He looked at the rim, then at her tense shoulders. Speed was a variable he respected, provided it didn’t compromise the physics of the repair. “Fast is fine, but rushed is how tubes get pinched. Grab a tire lever and pop one side of the tire bead off first.”

She hooked the plastic lever into the groove and yanked. Rubber screeched against steel. “Lever’s in my hand. I’m pulling the sidewall hard and it’s fighting me.”

“~~Don’t pull the rubber; slide the lever under the bead. Work around the circumference in six-inch increments.~~ **Don’t pull the rubber. Slide the lever under the bead and work your way around, six inches at a time.**”

She adjusted her grip, using leverage instead of brute force. The tire sighed as it peeled away from the steel. “Okay, one side is loose. Do I rip the whole thing off or just enough to get the tube?”

“~~Just enough to pinch and pull the valve straight out. Check the inside of the tire for thorns or shards before you put the new one in.~~ **Just enough to pinch and pull the valve straight out. Look inside the tire for thorns or glass before the new one goes in.**”

She ran a careful finger along the inner casing. “Clean.” She unrolled the fresh tube, stretching it taut. “I’m stretching the new tube and feeding it in. It feels tight.”

“~~That’s good tension, but don’t over-stretch. Seat the valve first, then tuck the rest of the tube evenly into the tire casing.~~ **Tension’s fine, just don’t overdo it. Seat the valve first, then tuck the rest of the tube in evenly.**”

She worked the rubber into the casing, feeling the smooth slide of latex against fabric. “Tube is in. Now I’m squeezing the tire back onto the rim. It’s resisting near the valve.”

Pranav stepped closer, eyes narrowing slightly at the critical junction. “~~That’s the danger zone. Use your palms to roll the bead on, not your fingers. If it catches, back off and reseat the tube.~~ **Yeah, that’s where it gets pinched. Use your palms to roll the bead on, not your fingers. If it catches, back off and reseat the tube.**”

She used the broad surface of her hands, rolling the final inch of rubber over the rim’s edge. “It’s on. I’m inflating to ten PSI. Does it look round?”

“~~Yes, perfect.~~ **Yep, looks round.**” He watched the tire expand, checking for any irregular bulges. “~~Now tighten the valve nut just enough to secure it, but don’t overtighten or you’ll strip the threads.~~ **Now snug up the valve nut, but not too hard or you’ll strip the threads.**”

She twisted the small brass nut with her thumbnail. “Done. Pumping to ninety PSI. No hissing, no bulges. You’re a lifesaver, Pranav.”

“~~Glad it’s holding. Check the pressure once more after you ride a block, just to be sure. Safe trip to your committee.~~ **Good, it’s holding. Check the pressure again after a block or so. Have fun at your committee.**”

Elif grabbed her handlebars, the bike suddenly whole again. “Will do. Thanks for keeping it simple. I’ll buy you a coffee from the break room later.”

“~~Sounds fair. Just leave the tools hanging; we like them tidy when people leave. See you around the co-op.~~ **Sounds fair. Hang the tools back up on your way out. See you around the co-op.**”

She slung her bag over her shoulder, nodding once. “You got it. Bye.”

Pranav watched her wheel the bike out into the sunlight, then turned back to the pegboard. “Bye.”

## B1445 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Genevieve

*2 edits applied, 0 rejected; 1.4% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Nikola sat on an overturned milk crate, the concrete floor cold through his trousers, staring at a clipboard that felt like an accusation. The air in the narrow storage closet was thick with the scent of zinc dust and old hydraulic oil. A flickering fluorescent tube buzzed overhead, casting jittery shadows across the rows of gray metal cabinets. One door hung open, revealing a tangle of shackles and chain coiled like sleeping snakes. He needed to finish this inspection template before the crew chief arrived in twenty minutes; a missing checkbox could ground the entire lighting plot.

Genevieve leaned against a high shelf, arms crossed, watching his frantic scribbling with mild amusement. She held a heavy two-ton chain hoist she had just pulled from the rack, its metal casing dull under the harsh light. She knew the checklist by heart, but she refused to coddle his panic. She wanted him to understand the mechanics, not just the paperwork.

Nikola looked up, eyes wide. “Genevieve, I need a chain hoist inspection checklist. The crew chief arrives in twenty and I’m drowning in boilerplate. Just give me the critical items.”

She stepped forward, extending the heavy tool toward him. “~~Sure. Grab that two-ton hoist hanging on the right. We’ll do it by the object so you don’t miss anything.~~ **Fine. Grab that two-ton hoist hanging on the right. We’ll go piece by piece, so nothing gets missed.**”

He took it, the weight surprising him. It felt solid, inert. “Okay, I’ve got it. It feels solid. What’s the first thing I’m supposed to look for?”

Genevieve pointed to the linked metal ribbon. “The chain itself. Unspool a few feet. Look for kinks, rust, or twisted links. If a link isn’t round, it’s dead weight.”

Nikola traced a finger along the links, frowning at a patch of orange discoloration. “Twisted links are obvious. What about surface rust? We’re in a storage closet, not a vault.”

“Surface rust is fine. Pitting or deep corrosion is not. Also, check the pitch. If the chain is stretched and loose between links, replace it.”

He nodded, marking the page with a sharp scratch of his pen. “Got it. Chain condition: round, no pits, no stretch. What’s next?”

Genevieve pulled the release lever, letting the hook drop a few inches before catching it. “The brake mechanism. Pull the release lever and let the hoist drop freely. It should stop immediately when you let go of the chain.”

He mimicked the motion, watching the hook sway. “If it slips a few inches before stopping, is that a fail?”

“Any movement beyond a link or two after release is a fail. The brake is the only thing keeping actors off the floor.”

Nikola’s hand tightened on the handle. “Noted. Brake engagement: instant stop. Now the hooks?”

“Check the safety latches on both the load hook and the upper eye hook. They must spring back instantly and close flat.”

He glanced at his draft, where he had written a vague instruction. “My draft said ‘verify latch integrity.’ Is that too vague?”

“Yes. ‘Spring back instantly’ is testable. ‘Integrity’ is a wish. Be specific.”

He crossed out the old line. “Fair. Latches spring and close flat. What about the housing or gear case?”

“Cracks in the cast housing mean instant discard. Also, check for oil leaks from the gear case.”

Nikola hesitated, weighing the severity. “Leaks are a maintenance issue, not a safety critical?”

“Leaks mean lubrication loss, which means brake failure. It’s safety critical. Add it.”

He added the item, the list now tight and precise. “Okay. Housing cracks, oil leaks, brake, chain, latches. That’s five items.”

Genevieve lifted the hoist, demonstrating the retraction. “Add one more: load chain travel. Ensure the chain retracts fully and doesn’t jam inside the case.”

“Jammed chain is rare but catastrophic. I’ll add that. Six items total.”

She handed the hoist back to him, her expression neutral. “~~Perfect. That’s your checklist. Sign it, date it, and don’t skip the brake test.~~ **That’s it, that’s your checklist. Sign it, date it, and don’t skip the brake test.**”

Nikola signed his name, the ink bold against the paper. “Won’t. Thanks for cutting through the noise. I’ll tape this to the clipboard now.”

Genevieve hung the hoist back on its hook with a satisfied click. The fluorescent bulb buzzed on, steady and indifferent.

The garage air hung thick with the scent of cured PVA and dried pine, illuminated by a single shaft of dust-moted sunlight cutting through the high window. Salvador stood hunched over the workbench, his tweezers poised like surgical instruments over a diorama of miniature snow-dusted cottages. Dark circles bruised the skin under his eyes; the deadline for the holiday card shoot loomed like a guillotine. He stared at a pile of white powder that clumped in uneven, heavy ridges. It looked less like a winter landscape and more like a kitchen accident.

Salvador spoke first, his voice tight with the friction of impending failure. “This cornstarch is clumping in the light. It looks like spilled flour, not snow, and I need it to sit on the roof tiles without sliding.”

Parisa leaned against the doorframe, clipboard tucked under one arm. She watched him for a beat, noting the tension in his jaw, before stepping into the light. Her expression was calm, analytical. She saw not a disaster, but a material science problem waiting for a variable adjustment.

“~~Cornstarch is too heavy for micro-details unless you modify the density. Have you tried mixing it with a tiny bit of white glitter?~~ **Cornstarch is just too heavy for tiny details, honestly. You could cut it with a pinch of white glitter, though. Tried that?**”

Salvador shook his head, the movement sharp. “Glitter catches too much light. It looks like confetti, not weather. I need matte white texture that reads as cold.”

Parisa nodded, accepting the constraint. She ran a finger along the edge of the bench, thinking. “~~Then switch to cellulose insulation. It’s fibrous, lightweight, and catches the directional light better than powder.~~ **Then use cellulose insulation. It’s fibrous and light, and it catches your directional light way better than powder does.**”

Salvador looked at the empty shelf where insulation should have been, then back at the jar of cornstarch. “I don’t have time to strip a whole panel of insulation. Do you have a quick hack for the powder I already have?”

Parisa pointed to the wire mesh strainer sitting near the coffee maker. “~~Sieve the cornstarch through two layers of tulle. It removes the fine dust that creates haze and leaves uniform particles.~~ **Run the cornstarch through two layers of tulle. That gets rid of the fine dust, which is what’s making the haze, and you end up with even particles.**”

Salvador grabbed the strainer and a scrap of fabric from his pocket. “Okay, sieved and uniform. But how do I get it to stick to the vertical surfaces without it falling off in frame one?”

Parisa reached into her pocket and produced a small, deflated latex balloon. “~~Use a static charge. Rub a balloon on your hair, hover it near the set, then tap the snow gently. The particles will cling to the roof.~~ **Static. Rub a balloon on your hair, hold it near the set, then tap the snow gently. It’ll cling to the roof.**”

Salvador stared at the balloon, incredulous. “Balloon hair tricks? This is professional stop-motion, not a science fair project. Is there a more industrial adhesive?”

Parisa held his gaze, unwavering. “~~You can mist the roof with diluted white glue, but that changes the texture. The static method preserves the matte finish. Trust the physics.~~ **You could mist the roof with watered-down white glue, but it changes the texture. Static keeps the matte finish. Trust me on this one.**”

Salvador exhaled, a long, slow release of breath that seemed to deflate his shoulders. He took the balloon. “Fine. One balloon, two sieves, and hope the static holds for the twenty-four frames I need. Let’s test it.”

Parisa moved to the lighting rig, adjusting the angle with practiced precision. “~~I’ll hold the lights steady. Go for a high angle shot; that’s where the clumping usually reveals itself.~~ **I’ve got the lights. Go for a high angle shot, since that’s where clumping usually shows up.**”

Salvador rubbed the balloon vigorously against his hair, the sound like dry leaves. He hovered it over the rooftops, sprinkling the sieved powder. “If this looks like dust again, I’m scrapping the whole scene. Ready on my mark. Three, two, one.”

Parisa squinted through the viewfinder. The individual flakes settled, suspended in a delicate lattice of static charge. “~~Hold. The shadow play is perfect now. The individual flakes are visible without the haze. You’re good to animate.~~ **Hold it there. Look at those shadows, you can see every flake and no haze. You’re good to animate.**”

Salvador lowered the tweezers, the knot in his chest loosening. “Okay, that actually worked. Thanks. I owe you a coffee. Black, no sugar, just like my mood right now.”

Parisa offered a small, satisfied smile. “~~Coffee later. For now, lock that frame and keep the balloon charged. You’ve got your snow.~~ **Coffee later. Lock that frame and keep the balloon charged. You’ve got your snow.**”

## B1896 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Yori

Adelina pressed the tape measure against the peeling paint, the latex scent of the ward thick in her nose. The whiteboard on wheels sat silent nearby, a stark white island in the dim alcove. She held a roll of poster tack like a weapon, her knuckles white.

Adelina: I have forty butterflies and a four-foot wall section above the bed rail. How do I arrange them so it looks calming and not like a chaotic swarm?

Yori leaned against the doorframe, clipboard tucked under his arm. He watched her squint at the wall, then tapped his pen against his chin.

Yori: Start with the negative space. Do not fill the entire area. Aim for a loose cloud formation centered at eye level when the patient is sitting up, not lying flat.

She paced the narrow strip of floor, measuring with her eyes. The room felt too small for ambition.

Adelina: Eye level when sitting is roughly five feet up. If I space them evenly, it looks rigid. I need organic flow but without the mess.

He stepped into the light, gesturing with the pen toward the ceiling line.

Yori: Use a triangular anchor. Place three butterflies at the highest points, then cascade downward in staggered rows, leaving at least two inches of empty wall between every pair.

Adelina frowned, picking up a lavender butterfly and holding it up. The paper felt fragile, the wings crisp.

Adelina: Two inches seems sparse for a full wall. Won’t it look half-finished if I stop halfway down?

Yori shook his head slowly, his expression unreadable but firm.

Yori: It will look curated. Overcrowding increases cognitive load in small spaces. Trust the white space; it lets the colors breathe.

She let the butterfly drop back into the bin. The plastic clinked softly. She looked at the adhesive strips on the table, then at the bin of delicate paper wings.

Adelina: Okay, staggered rows. Now, the mounting. These are thin paper. If I use heavy-duty adhesive, they’ll tear when I inevitably have to rotate the display.

Yori reached into his pocket and pulled out a small dispenser of mini-strips.

Yori: Use 3M Command mini-strips with the low-tack backing. Apply one strip to the upper left wing and one to the lower right for balance.

Adelina groaned, rubbing her temple. The fluorescent hum seemed to spike.

Adelina: Two strips per butterfly? That’s a lot of labor for a temporary install. Can I just use double-sided tape on the folds?

Yori held up a finger, stopping her argument before it started.

Yori: Tape on the folds weakens the crease structure. The butterflies will droop within a week. The strips add weight distribution that keeps the wings crisp.

She sighed, the sound sharp in the quiet room. She picked up a blue butterfly, feeling the precision of the fold.

Adelina: Fine, strips it is. What about color distribution? I have a mix of blues and yellows. Do I alternate them strictly?

Yori smiled faintly, a small crack in his professional demeanor.

Yori: Group them softly. Put three blues together, then two yellows, then back to blues. Strict alternation feels clinical; soft clusters feel natural and restful.

Adelina nodded, the tension leaving her shoulders. She placed the first blue butterfly on the imaginary line, visualizing the cloud taking shape. The room suddenly felt larger, the air lighter. She reached for the next strip, her hands steady now.

## B2101 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Sakura

*10 edits applied, 0 rejected; 8.2% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 3 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Gene tapped his pen against the folding table, the plastic surface vibrating with the rhythmic thud. The basement air was thick with the scent of stale popcorn and industrial floor cleaner, a haze that seemed to cling to the rows of mismatched tablecloths. On his laptop screen, the chaotic syntax of a GitHub Actions workflow file glowed, a visual representation of his mounting anxiety. He didn’t have time for theoretical lectures; he needed a binary answer before the next sprint.

“Hey, I need to vet code-scanners for this nonprofit site. Team is small, budget is zero. Which tool actually catches real bugs without breaking our build every Tuesday?”

Sakura unscrewed the cap of her thermos, the faint aroma of matcha cutting through the stale atmosphere. She didn’t look at her phone, but kept her eyes on Gene’s tense shoulders, sliding a printed comparison sheet across the table. “~~For a lean team, start with Semgrep. It’s fast, runs locally, and catches common web vulns without the heavy infrastructure SonarQube demands.~~ **Small team? Start with Semgrep. It’s fast, runs locally, and catches the common web vulns without all the infrastructure SonarQube wants.**”

Gene picked up the sheet, scanning the bullet points with a skeptical squint. “Semgrep sounds like a marketing name. Is it just regex with a pulse, or does it understand control flow?”

“~~It uses pattern matching with data-flow analysis, so it tracks variables across lines.~~ **Nah, it does pattern matching plus data-flow analysis, so it follows variables across lines.**” She tapped the paper lightly. “~~Better than regex, lighter than a full SAST suite.~~ **Way better than regex, and lighter than a full SAST suite.**”

He leaned back, the chair legs scraping against the concrete. “Okay, but Bandit is free and built for Python. Why not just use that since we’re on Django?”

“~~Bandit is great for syntax-level issues, but it misses logic flaws.~~ **Bandit’s fine for syntax-level stuff, but it misses logic flaws.**” Sakura took a slow sip, her demeanor calm despite the humming servers overhead. “~~Use Bandit for a quick pass, but Semgrep catches the business-logic injections you’re worried about.~~ **Use Bandit for a quick pass. Semgrep’s the one that catches the business-logic injections you’re worried about.**”

Gene rubbed his temple, the headache behind his eyes pulsing in time with the projector’s flicker. “So I run both? That feels like double maintenance. I want one tool that doesn’t require a PhD to configure.”

“~~You can integrate both into GitHub Actions.~~ **Put both in GitHub Actions.**” She pointed to the workflow diagram on his screen. “~~Bandit takes seconds; Semgrep takes thirty. Run Bandit first for fast fail, then Semgrep for depth.~~ **Bandit takes seconds, Semgrep about thirty. Run Bandit first so it fails fast, then Semgrep.**”

“Thirty seconds is fine if it doesn’t spam false positives.” He gestured vaguely at the server racks in the corner, as if they might judge his failure. “My team ignores alerts after the first week of noise.”

“~~Semgrep lets you whitelist rules easily. Start with the ‘default’ config, which is conservative. Add custom rules only for your specific Django patterns.~~ **Whitelisting rules in Semgrep is easy. Start with the ’default’ config, it’s conservative. Only add custom rules for your own Django patterns.**”

Gene stared at the blinking cursor on his screen, imagining the clean, quiet build pipeline Sakura described. “And if I find a critical flaw, how do I know it’s not a ghost? I can’t afford to stop deployment for a false alarm.”

“~~Semgrep marks findings with confidence levels and links to CWEs.~~ **Semgrep tags findings with confidence levels and links the CWEs.**” She closed her thermos with a decisive click. “~~Verify manually only if it’s ‘High’ severity and touches user input.~~ **Only check by hand if it’s ’High’ severity and touches user input.**”

He stopped tapping his pen. The knot in his chest loosened, just a fraction. “Alright. I’ll add both to the workflow, whitelist the noise, and trust the defaults. Thanks, that saves me a week of reading docs.”

Sakura nodded, already reaching for her bag. Gene turned back to his laptop, the code no longer looking like a minefield, but a manageable list of tasks.

## B2297 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Oona

*3 edits applied, 1 rejected; 1.4% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Tadhg stood knee-deep in a tangle of black plastic sheeting, the damp cardboard smell of the set clinging to his glitter-stained hoodie. The gym loomed above him, a cavern of flickering construction lights and half-built papier-mâché tombstones. He tapped his clipboard against his palm, the audio cue list looking less like a plan and more like a wish list given the budget. Beside a stack of cinder blocks, Oona balanced a laptop on her knee, her flannel sleeves rolled up to reveal forearms tense with calculation. She had spent the hour measuring the acoustic decay of the high ceiling, knowing the hard surfaces would amplify every mistake.

Tadhg broke the silence, gesturing toward the scavenged equipment. “I’ve got twelve cheap Bluetooth speakers and one wall-mounted PA. Can we make this hallway sound haunted without sounding like a broken iPod?”

Oona didn’t look up from her screen, her eyes tracking waveforms that mirrored the room’s geometry. “It’s all about placement and negative space. Don’t try to fill the room; make the empty spots hurt. Start by clustering four small speakers in the far corner, facing each other.”

Tadhg frowned, visualizing the setup amidst the fog machines. “Facing each other? Won’t that just cancel out the bass?”

“Exactly.” She finally looked at him, her expression calm. “You want phase cancellation for the low end—it creates a sub-bass rumble you feel more than hear. It’s cheap visceral fear.”

He nodded, the tension in his shoulders easing slightly. “Okay, so the corner does the physical dread. What about the directional scares? I need something that feels like it’s walking past you.”

Oona tapped a rhythm on her thigh, mimicking a footstep. “Use the wall-mounted PA for the wide ambience, but delay the small speakers by forty milliseconds. It tricks the brain into thinking the sound source is moving through the space.”

“Forty milliseconds? How precise do I need to be with the delay?”

“Precise enough to be annoying if you’re close, immersive if you’re walking. Use a simple free app to sync the playback start times.”

Tadhg let out a short, dry laugh, kicking at a loose cable. “I don’t have an app for that. I have a laptop and a tangled mess of Aux cables.”

Oona shrugged, already adjusting a slider on her interface. “Then split the signal from the laptop. Use a cheap Y-adapter. Send left to the corner cluster, right to the PA, and mute the center channel entirely.”

“Mute the center? That leaves a dead zone in the middle of the hall.”

“No, it leaves a vacuum. Human hearing hates vacuum. We’ll fill it with intermittent silence—two seconds of sound, three seconds of nothing.”

Tadhg rubbed his chin, the dust making his skin itch. “Intermittent silence feels risky. If they stop walking, the spell breaks.”

“Only if they notice the silence is artificial. Layer a very low-frequency drone under the silence. Barely audible, just enough to keep the tension high.”

“So the drone is the glue? I can generate that in Audacity in five minutes.”

“~~Yes. Export it as a loop. Keep the volume low on the small speakers so they only fire when someone is within three meters.~~ **Yeah. Export it as a loop. Keep the small speakers quiet so they only fire when someone’s within three meters.**”

“And the wall speaker? Does it just play the whole track?”

“No. The wall speaker plays the room tone—the wind, the creaks. The small speakers are the ghosts. Keep the source distinct from the atmosphere.”

Tadhg exhaled, the rigid line of his posture softening as the plan coalesced. “Atmosphere on the wall, ghosts in the corners. Low bass rumble, delayed panning, silent gaps. Write that down for me?”

Oona printed the summary. “1. Corner cluster: Phase-cancel bass, 40ms delay. 2. Wall PA: Room tone only. 3. Gaps: 2s sound, 3s silence + sub-drone.” She handed him the slip. “~~Simple, effective.~~ **Easy enough.**”

“Perfect. I’ll run the cables now. Thanks for saving me from a stereo setup.”

“~~Anytime.~~ **No worries.**” She closed the laptop lid with a click. “Just make sure the batteries are fresh; nothing kills fear faster than a dying speaker clicking.”

Tadhg grabbed the cables, the weight of the deadline lifting just enough to let him move. The gym’s shadows seemed less like empty space and more like waiting mouths.

## B2321 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Wilfred

*7 edits applied, 3 rejected; 6.6% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → yes, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Anastasia stood over the open crate, the tablet’s screen casting a harsh blue glow on the dense white foam cradling the bronze bust. The loading dock air was crisp, tasting of diesel and wet concrete. Forty minutes until the van arrived, and the data on her screen made her fingers tense around the edges of the device. She looked up as Wilfred stepped out from the shadow of the roller shutter, his arms crossing over his chest in a gesture of calm assessment.

“Wilfred, we’re running out of time. I just pulled the last mile’s accelerometer log from the test rig, and I see a 4.2g spike. Is that safe for this bronze bust, or do I need to repack the foam right now?”

Wilfred shook his head slowly, stepping closer to peer at the jagged line. “~~Hold on, don’t touch the foam yet. Peak force is only half the story. Pull up the frequency graph on your tablet—what’s the dominant Hz range during that bump?~~ **Hold on, don’t touch the foam yet. Peak force is only half of it. Pull up the frequency graph. What’s the dominant Hz range during that bump?**”

Anastasia tapped the screen, zooming in on the chaotic waveforms. “It’s messy, but the spikes cluster around forty-five Hertz. That’s the wheel hop from the pothole. Why does that matter?”

Wilfred pointed a finger at the bronze face buried in the packing peanuts. “~~Bronze has a natural resonance near fifty Hertz. If you hit that sweet spot, even a 2g shock can cause micro-fractures in the patina. Forty-five is close enough to worry.~~ **Bronze rings near fifty Hertz. Hit that sweet spot and even a 2g shock can micro-fracture the patina. Forty-five’s close enough to worry me.**”

She exhaled, her shoulders dropping slightly as the panic receded into calculation. “So the 4.2g isn’t the problem, the frequency is. I thought I just needed to keep the g’s under five.”

“~~Under five is for stone or dense ceramics. Metals sing.~~ **Under five’s fine for stone or dense ceramics. Metals sing.**” He reached into his back pocket, producing a yellowed, dog-eared clipboard. “~~You need to dampen the high-frequency jitter, not just cushion the big hits. Look at my clipboard here, red line for bronze is 3g average, but strict on resonance.~~ **You’ve got to kill the high-frequency jitter, not just cushion the big hits. See my clipboard? Red line for bronze is 3g average, but it’s strict on resonance.**”

Anastasia scanned the faded handwriting, the urgency of the clock ticking in her head. “I don’t have time to swap out the foam for a resonant-dampening gel. The van is idling. Is there a quick fix with the vehicle?”

Wilfred nodded, tapping the clipboard with his thumb. “~~Yes. Tell the driver to engage the air-ride suspension’s ‘fragile’ mode. It softens the rear axles and cuts high-frequency transmission by nearly half.~~ **Yeah. Tell the driver to switch the air-ride suspension to ’fragile’ mode. It softens the rear axles and cuts high-frequency transmission by nearly half.**”

Anastasia considered the trade-off. “That’ll slow us down, but it might work. Will it drop the effective g-force enough to stay clear of the resonance zone?”

“It should.” He gestured toward the waiting van, its engine idling low. “The softer mount isolates the crate from the road’s chatter. You’ll still feel the bump, but the crate won’t feel the ring.”

She nodded, already mentally drafting the text to the driver. “Okay, I’ll instruct the driver. But I want to verify. Can I tape a second sensor to the crate itself to confirm the isolation works?”

Wilfred smiled, a small, approving quirk of his lips. “~~Smart move. Tape one to the base of the bust, not the crate wall. That tells you what the artifact actually feels.~~ **Good thinking. Tape one to the base of the bust, not the crate wall. That’s what the artifact actually feels.**”

Anastasia fished a fresh sensor from her belt pouch, peeling the adhesive backing. “Done. If the crate sensor shows 4g but the bust shows 1.5g, we’re good to go?”

“Exactly.” He leaned against the crate edge, watching her work. “If the ratio is less than three-to-one, you’re playing with fire. Three-to-one or better, and you’re safe.”

“Three-to-one.” She pressed the sensor firmly against the bronze base, checking the alignment. “Got it. I’m sticking the sensor on now. Thanks for stopping me from over-packing that foam for nothing.”

Wilfred pushed off the crate, turning back toward the shadows of the shutter. “~~Anytime. Just make sure that sensor cable doesn’t snag on the liftgate. See you at the gallery unloading.~~ **Sure. Just don’t let that sensor cable snag on the liftgate. See you at the gallery unloading.**”

## B2652 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Isolde

*0 edits applied, 7 rejected; 0.0% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The amber cone of Mirek’s work lamp cut through the stagnant air of the St. Jude Heritage Library, illuminating dust motes that drifted like suspended ash. The silence here was heavy, pressurized by the locked iron gates and the towering cliffs of leather-bound folios that loomed in the shadows. The smell of lignin decay and floor wax coated the back of his throat. Mirek leaned over the CAD floor plan spread across the heavy oak table, his eyes red-rimmed and dry from hours of staring at screens. He rubbed his temple, then pointed a weary finger at the west aisle on the schematic.

Mirek: Isolde, I’m stuck. The west aisle needs coverage, but the heritage clause says no drill holes in the limestone. I need a mount that’s invisible but holds three kilos of camera gear.

Isolde sat opposite him, the rhythmic *shhh-shhh* of her bone folder against a whetstone the only other sound. She didn’t look up immediately, finishing the edge of the tool before setting it down with deliberate calm. Her gaze fixed on the blueprints, then on Mirek’s frustrated posture.

Isolde: You can’t just stick a PTZ unit to a two-hundred-year-old wall and hope it stays. What adhesive are you proposing?

Mirek tapped the table near the aisle’s boundary. He pictured the clean, modern line of a camera disappearing into the historic gloom.

Mirek: 3M VHB foam tape. It’s rated for glass and steel, should be fine on stone. I’ll clean the surface with alcohol.

Isolde shook her head, a slight, knowing motion. She gestured toward the far wall, where the old cast-iron radiator stood silent and cold.

Isolde: Alcohol will strip the wax coating off that limestone, and VHB fails in cold drafts. The radiator vent there creates micro-climates. It will peel within a month.

Mirek sighed, running a hand through his hair. The idea of starting over filled him with a heavy dread.

Mirek: Great. So what’s your genius idea? I don’t have time for a custom bracket fabrication cycle.

Isolde leaned forward, her voice dropping to a conversational murmur that seemed to absorb the room’s echo. She traced a line on the paper with her fingernail.

Isolde: Use the existing cast-iron radiator pipe. It runs vertically right behind the bookshelf. Clamp a thin rail to the pipe, run the cable inside.

Mirek frowned, picturing the grimy, curved metal. It seemed fragile, a relic not meant for modern weight.

Mirek: The pipe is narrow, curved, and covered in grime. Clamping to it risks damaging the enamel finish.

Isolde reached into her satchel and pulled out a small roll of clear silicone tubing, holding it up to the lamp light. It was flexible, translucent.

Isolde: We use a soft silicone sleeve around the clamp, not metal-on-metal. The pipe is structural steel; it won’t budge. It’s already there, Mirek.

Mirek looked at the tubing, then back at the plan. The logistics of threading cable through old plumbing gnawed at him.

Mirek: Running cable down inside a pipe sounds like a nightmare to fish later. If the camera fails, I’m crawling in there?

Isolde smiled faintly, a teacher’s expression of patience. She tapped the diagram again.

Isolde: Leave a pull string. And the camera face mounts to the rail, so you access the lens from outside. It’s cleaner than drilling.

Mirek rubbed his jaw, the CAD time required flashing through his mind. He needed a specific angle, a precise tilt.

Mirek: I’d need a custom L-bracket to hit the angle. I don’t have the CAD time for that.

Isolde nodded, already visualizing the solution. She pointed toward the door leading to the repair lab.

Isolde: Use an adjustable camera shoe plate. You can tilt it without modifying the bracket. I have one in the repair lab.

Mirek hesitated, then looked at the edge of the heavy oak shelving unit depicted on the plan. A simpler, if riskier, alternative formed in his mind.

Mirek: If I drill into the shelf side instead? Just a small anchor in the wood?

Isolde’s expression hardened slightly. She knew the history of those shelves, the delicate tension holding them in place.

Isolde: The shelving is suspended, not fixed. You’ll pull the whole unit out of alignment. The pipe is the only load-bearing path.

Mirek stared at the blue lines of the pipe route, the silence stretching between them. He conceded with a sharp exhale, his shoulders dropping.

Mirek: Fine. I’ll rig the silicone sleeve. But if the vibration from the radiator messes with the focus, I’m blaming you.

Isolde stood, gathering her bone folder. She offered a small, reassuring nod.

Isolde: Vibration is negligible at this flow rate. Go get the pull string, and I’ll bring the shoe plate. We’ll have it live by tomorrow.

Mirek pushed his chair back, the legs scraping softly against the waxed floor. He turned toward the shelves, the problem solved, the work beginning again.

## B2687 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Noemi

*9 edits applied, 0 rejected; 17.7% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 3 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Kellan stood before the whiteboard, the dry-erase markers’ chemical tang mixing with the ozone smell of the label printers. He tapped his tablet against his palm, staring at a matrix of drop angles that felt more like a guess than a protocol. Noemi leaned against the steel calibration weights, her clipboard stained with coffee rings, watching him wrestle with the physics of falling cardboard. The rhythmic thud of the stress tester in the next bay provided a steady, indifferent heartbeat to the room.

“Noemi, do you have five minutes to sanity-check my drop-test matrix? I’m worried I’m over-engineering the variables and missing the obvious failure modes.”

She pushed off the weights, adjusting her grip on the clipboard. “~~Always happy to help. Send me the current plan? I’d rather look at the actual angles you’re proposing than guess what you’re thinking.~~ **Sure. Show me the plan. I’d rather see the actual angles than guess.**”

Kellan extended the tablet, the screen glowing harshly in the LED light. “I’ve got flat-face, edge, and corner drops at 1.2 meters. Is that sufficient for a standard e-commerce box, or am I leaving a gap in coverage?”

Noemi scanned the list, her eyes narrowing slightly at the symmetry. “~~That covers the basics, but you’re missing the 'worst-case' scenario. For a rectangular prism, the corner drops are critical because the energy disperses unevenly. Make sure you’re testing all three principal axes.~~ **That’s the basics, but you’re missing worst case. On a rectangular box the corner drops matter most, since the energy spreads unevenly. Hit all three principal axes.**”

He frowned, the friction of the eraser already audible in his head. “All three? That doubles the run count. If I rotate the box for every corner, I’ll burn through my budget of samples before Tuesday.”

She shook her head, a small, patient gesture. “~~You don’t need every permutation if the package is symmetrical. Just pick the most likely impact orientation based on how it sits on the conveyor belt. Focus on the center of gravity.~~ **Not if the package is symmetrical. Pick the orientation it’s most likely to land on, going by how it sits on the conveyor. Think center of gravity.**”

Kellan nodded, mentally deleting half the spreadsheet. “Okay, so I prioritize the bottom corners. What about the surface? The spec says 'concrete,' but the lab floor is epoxy over steel. Does that change the G-force spike?”

Noemi pointed to the drop-test sled’s gleaming hydraulic arm. “~~Yes, significantly. Steel is non-yielding, which maximizes the shock transmission. If you want real-world relevance, use a concrete slab. Steel gives you a 'best-case' for durability, not a realistic one.~~ **Yeah, a lot. Steel doesn’t give at all, so you get the most shock transmission. If you want it realistic, use a concrete slab. Steel’s a best case for durability, not a real one.**”

He glanced at the polished floor, then at the crate of poly-mailers. “We don’t have a concrete slab in this bay. Can I use a rubberized mat to simulate a softer landing?”

“~~Only if you’re testing for cushioning performance. For structural integrity, stick to the hardest surface available. It isolates the package strength from the floor’s forgiveness.~~ **Only if you’re testing the cushioning. For structural stuff, use the hardest surface you’ve got. Then it’s the package you’re testing, not how forgiving the floor is.**”

Kellan exhaled, the tension in his shoulders easing. “Got it. Hard surface, concrete equivalent. Now, pass/fail criteria. I’m looking for external damage and internal product fracture. Is visual inspection enough?”

Noemi tapped the clipboard with a pen. “~~Visual is a start, but you need data. Place an accelerometer in the package center. Look for peak Gs over 50. If the box survives but the internal damping fails, the customer still returns it.~~ **Visual’s a start, but you need numbers. Put an accelerometer in the middle of the package and watch for peak Gs over 50. Box survives but the damping fails inside, the customer still sends it back.**”

“Accelerometers cost money per drop. Can I just correlate the external dent depth to internal failure from previous trials?”

“~~You can try, but correlation isn’t causation. One outlier dent could hide a brittle failure in the insert. I’d recommend instrumenting at least one run per variable to establish a baseline.~~ **You can try, but one odd dent could hide a brittle failure in the insert. I’d instrument at least one run per variable to get a baseline.**”

He considered the trade-off, then nodded. “Fine. One instrumented run per angle to set the G-threshold, then visual checks for the rest. That keeps me within budget but scientifically sound.”

“~~Exactly. And remember to log the ambient temperature; cardboard stiffness changes with humidity. Keep it consistent, or your data will be noise.~~ **Right. And log the ambient temperature, since cardboard stiffness changes with humidity. Keep it steady or your data’s just noise.**”

Kellan looked at the thermostat on the far wall. “Noted. I’ll lock the HVAC at 22 degrees. Thanks, Noemi—this saved me from designing a test that proves nothing.”

Noemi offered a thin smile. “~~Anytime. Run the first drop, watch the telemetry, and call me if the numbers look weird. I’ll be right down the hall if you need a second pair of eyes.~~ **Sure. Run the first drop and watch the telemetry. If the numbers look weird, I’m down the hall.**”

He turned back to the sled, the weight of the decision lifting as the machinery hummed around him.
