## B2691 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Nereo

*5 edits applied, 2 rejected; 7.7% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 1 (before = R01's A2).*

The asphalt radiated heat in shimmering waves, warping the chalk outlines of vendor booths. The air hung thick with the scent of roasted nuts and the sharp, metallic tang of fresh ink. Mona sat behind her folding table, the black cloth wrinkled under her elbows. Her vintage Underwood typewriter rested on a wooden shipping crate, its keys yellowed by time and use. A stack of heavy cream cardstock waited, pristine and judging. She typed a line, hit the carriage return with a heavy *clack*, stared at the black ink, and sighed. The woman in the red scarf stood ten feet away, oblivious.

Mona tapped a key, then hesitated. “I’m stuck. I’ve typed three lines for that woman with the red scarf, and they’re all drivel. I need a system for doing this in under a minute.”

Nereo leaned against the frame of his calligraphy booth three stalls over. He held a charcoal sketchbook, eyes tracking the rhythm of Mona’s frustration. He watched her delete nothing—there was no backspace, only the permanent commitment of ink—and smiled faintly. “~~You’re trying to paint a portrait when you only have three brushstrokes. Stop counting syllables for a second and look at her. What’s the one thing that’s sharp?~~ **You’re trying to paint a portrait with three brushstrokes. Quit counting syllables and look at her. What’s the one sharp thing?**”

Mona adjusted her glasses, squinting against the glare. “The red scarf. But ‘red scarf blows’ is too simple. It feels lazy, like I’m not trying. I want it to feel crafted.”

Nereo turned a page in his sketchbook, the paper whispering. “~~Crafted doesn’t mean complex. It means precise. Drop the scarf. What’s she doing? She’s checking her watch, isn’t she?~~ **Crafted isn’t complex. It’s precise. Forget the scarf. What’s she doing? Checking her watch, right?**”

Mona glanced back. The woman’s wrist was indeed bare, watch face up. “She is. But ‘watch checks time’ is redundant. How do I make that poetic without taking five minutes to think of a metaphor?”

“Juxtaposition.” Nereo tapped his pen against the book’s edge. “Two images, no bridge. ‘Red silk’ then ‘ticking face.’ You don’t need to explain the connection; the reader does the work. That’s fast.”

Mona frowned, her finger hovering over the space bar. “That’s abstract. If I type that, she might think I’m mocking her. I need it to feel personal, not like a riddle.”

“~~Specificity creates intimacy.~~ **Get specific and it gets personal.**” Nereo leaned closer, voice dropping to a conversational murmur. “~~Add a concrete detail. Not just red, but ‘frayed red silk.’ Not just ticking, but ‘cold steel tick.’ Concrete nouns ground it.~~ **Give her something you can touch. Not just red, ’frayed red silk.’ Not just ticking, ’cold steel tick.’ Solid nouns hold it down.**”

Mona considered this, the mechanical clatter of nearby booths fading into background noise. She looked at the scarf, then the watch. “Okay. ‘Frayed red silk / cold steel tick / wind steals the afternoon.’ Does that work? It feels faster to assemble.”

Nereo nodded, satisfied. “~~That’s a haiku. You cut the fluff. Next time, grab the texture and the object. Skip the feeling; the reader feels it.~~ **Look at that, a haiku. No fluff. Next time grab the texture and the object, and leave the feeling out. They’ll feel it.**”

Mona exhaled, a tension leaving her shoulders. She aligned a fresh sheet of cardstock. “Texture and object. Got it. No feelings, just things that clash. Thanks, I’ll try that on the next guy.”

Nereo pushed off the table, returning to his sketches. “Good luck. And remember, if the typewriter jams, just walk away. The next passerby will be worth more anyway.”

Mona watched him go, then looked up. A man in a suit was approaching, checking his own phone. She rolled the platen, the mechanism smooth and sure, and waited for the moment to strike.

Faye pressed her thumb against the throttle trim, her knuckles white, fighting a tremor that had nothing to do with the wind whipping grit across the tarmac. The air tasted of hot lithium polymer batteries and burnt solder, a familiar perfume that suddenly felt suffocating. On the reinforced landing pad beside her, her rig sat idle, its propellers still, while her mind raced through the implications of the sleek, unmarked black telemetry box sitting on the folding table.

Makoto leaned against that table, sipping from a dented thermos. He watched the dust swirl around the concrete barriers, his gaze calm and methodical. He saw Faye’s distress not as drama, but as a structural weakness in their operation, one that needed immediate shoring up before the whole framework collapsed.

Faye spoke first, her voice tight. “Makoto, I need you to look at this telemetry unit from our new sponsor. They want us to flash it into the main flight controller before Saturday.”

Makoto set the thermos down with a soft clink. His eyes narrowed, tracking the device’s unbranded casing. “That black box isn’t standard CIB. It’s a custom voltage regulator bypassing the ESC cutoffs. You’re looking at a safety violation.”

She picked at a grease stain on her oversized team jacket sleeve. “They claim it’s just an extended runtime mod for endurance runs. They’re threatening to pull the grant if we don’t test it.”

Makoto shook his head, a single, sharp motion. “It’s not a mod; it’s a cheat. If a crash happens and that device is logged, your license is gone, not just the money.”

The wind howled through the pop-up tents, rattling their plastic seams. Faye rubbed her temples. “I can’t afford to lose the sponsorship, but I can’t fly a bomb. How do I say no without sounding like I’m refusing work?”

“~~Don’t refuse the work; refuse the risk.~~ **Don’t turn down the work. Turn down the risk.**” Makoto gestured to the cracked spine of the official race rulebook on the table. “~~Quote Rule 4.2 on electronic integrity. Make it about liability, not morality.~~ **Quote Rule 4.2, the electronic integrity one. Keep it about liability, not morals.**”

Faye frowned, the logic snagging in her anxiety. “I don’t want to lecture them on ethics. They care about results, not rules.”

“They care about insurance.” He tapped the cover of the rulebook, then pointed to a clause near the back. “~~Tell them the insurer won’t cover flights with unauthorized firmware. Shift the blame to the policy.~~ **Tell them the insurer won’t cover flights with unauthorized firmware. Let the policy take the heat.**”

A small breath escaped her, lighter than before. “That’s clean. Do I have to return the hardware now, or can I keep it for later?”

“~~Return it today.~~ **Send it back today.**” Makoto’s tone was flat, final. “~~Keep a photo of the serial number for your records. Then send a polite email citing the insurance clause.~~ **Snap a photo of the serial number first. Then email them, nicely, and point to the insurance clause.**”

She nodded, drafting the sentence in her head. “Okay. ‘Per insurer requirements, we cannot install non-certified hardware.’ That covers me?”

“~~Perfect.~~ **Yep, that works.**” He picked up the black box, weighing it lightly before setting it back down. “~~It’s boring, it’s true, and it stops the conversation. You keep the team legal and the sponsor looking reasonable.~~ **It’s boring and it’s true, and that ends the conversation. Team stays legal, sponsor still looks reasonable.**”

Faye exhaled, the tension in her shoulders dropping a fraction. “Thanks, Makoto. I was spiraling, but this feels like a solid exit ramp.”

“~~Glad to help.~~ **No problem.**” He reached past the rulebook, grabbing a soldering iron from the cluttered tray. “Now hand me that soldering iron; your motor mount is loose, and we don’t have time for that to fail.”

Faye passed the tool over, the heat of the barrel warming her palm, grounding her back in the mechanics of the machine, away from the politics.

## B2907 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Harlan

*10 edits applied, 1 rejected; 12.8% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 3 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The garage smelled of wet cardboard and the sharp, metallic tang of rusting rebar. Afternoon light filtered through high, grime-streaked windows, illuminating dust motes that danced over stacks of papier-mâché tubs and coils of chicken wire. In the center of the floor sat the six-foot fish head, its surface a patchwork of drying newsprint strips and rough plaster patches. Giselle stood on a stepladder, her knuckles white against the aluminum rungs, staring at the porous snout with deep concern. She needed the head to survive a drizzly parade route without slumping, but her academic budget could not afford professional marine-grade resins. She wanted a cheap, effective solution now, not a lecture on material science history.

Harlan leaned against a workbench cluttered with brushes and jars of pigment. He wore a smock stained with every color of the rainbow, his hands resting on a jar of gold leaf dust. He knew Giselle’s impatience; she checked her watch when he held a brush up for more than three seconds. He was happy to help but refused to offer advice that led to structural failure. He watched her squint at the snout, then lifted his chin toward the ladder.

Giselle spoke first, her voice tight with the ticking clock of the afternoon. “Hi Harlan. I need to waterproof this fish head for the parade tomorrow. It’s predicted to drizzle. Can I just coat it in matte acrylic medium and call it done?”

Harlan shook his head slowly, his eyes tracking the uneven texture of the plaster. “~~No, acrylic medium stays too permeable when saturated. It’ll turn to mush within an hour of heavy rain, and you’ll lose the structural integrity of the paper layers.~~ **Nope. Acrylic medium stays permeable once it’s soaked. An hour of heavy rain and it’s mush, and the paper layers go with it.**”

She tapped the ladder rung, frustration flaring in her jaw. “I’m on a zero-dollar budget for extra materials. I have latex caulk and some shellac. Which is less likely to cause a collapse?”

“~~Neither is ideal for a full coat, but shellac is brittle and cracks with flex.~~ **Neither’s great for a full coat, but shellac’s brittle. It cracks the second the thing flexes.**” He reached for a rag, wiping a smudge of blue from his thumb. “~~Use a dilute white glue mixture—five parts PVA to one part water—for a base seal, then top with polyurethane.~~ **Thin some white glue, five parts PVA to one part water, and brush that on as a base seal. Then polyurethane over the top.**”

Giselle scanned the shelves behind him, her eyes narrowing. “I don’t have polyurethane. I have spray-on varnish. Is that close enough?”

“~~If it’s acrylic-based spray varnish, no. It behaves like the matte medium.~~ **Not if it’s acrylic-based. That acts just like the matte medium.**” Harlan pointed a calloused finger toward the darker corner of the shop. “~~You need a solvent-based polyurethane spray. Check your inventory for Minwax or similar brands labeled 'exterior grade.'~~ **You want a solvent-based polyurethane spray. Look for Minwax or something similar that says ’exterior grade’ on it.**”

She disappeared into the shadows and returned a moment later, holding a red can that smelled faintly of turpentine even from a distance. “I found a can of spar urethane in the back. It’s oil-based and smells like a chemical plant. Is that safe for the paper?”

Harlan nodded, satisfied. “~~Spar urethane is perfect. It flexes with the humidity changes and creates a hard, waterproof skin. The smell is unfortunate, but it won’t degrade the paper if you spray light coats.~~ **Spar urethane’s perfect. It moves with the humidity and dries to a hard, waterproof skin. Yeah, it stinks, but it won’t hurt the paper as long as you keep the coats light.**”

Giselle shook the can, listening to the rattle of the mixing ball. “How many coats? I have forty-five minutes before they move the float to the truck.”

“~~Two light coats, spaced ten minutes apart.~~ **Two light coats, ten minutes apart.**” He stepped closer, gesturing to the curve of the fish’s cheek. “~~Do not flood it. Heavy application pools and drips, creating weak spots that peel off in the rain.~~ **Don’t flood it. Lay it on thick and it pools and drips, and those spots peel off in the rain.**”

“And if I miss a spot? Can I touch up with brush later?”

“~~Only if you’re careful. Brushing can disturb the paper fibers if they’re still soft. Spray is safer. Aim for even coverage, especially around the gills and eye sockets where water pools.~~ **Only if you go easy. Brushing can drag up the paper fibers while they’re still soft, so spray’s safer. Keep it even, especially around the gills and eye sockets, since water sits there.**”

Giselle took a deep breath, accepting the limitation. “Understood. Two light coats of spar urethane, ten minutes between. Thanks, Harlan.”

“~~You’re welcome.~~ **Sure.**” He watched her descend the ladder, his gaze dropping to the lower jaw. “Watch the drip lines on the jaw—that’s where the weight of the water will pull the paper apart first.”

The hiss of the spray can broke the silence, a fine mist coating the rough snout in a clear, protective film. Giselle worked quickly, the smell of solvent filling the space between them, but the fish held its shape, rigid and ready for the rain.

Naseem hesitated, then took the marker. She drew a thin, deliberate stroke across the quartz, the ink dark and wet against the clear plastic. “Like a capillary test. If the ink stays, it’s a breach. Okay, giving it a second.”

She grabbed a wipe, pressing it firmly along the line. “Wipe now. See if the black line remains embedded in the fracture.”

Naseem held the tip up to the harsh fluorescent light. The white wipe came away mostly clean, but a faint, jagged black line remained trapped in the quartz. “It’s faint, but the ink held. The crack penetrates the lens thickness. So it’s definitely damaged.”

Leni nodded, her expression serious. “~~Yes. A penetrated crack will scatter the blue spectrum and reduce cure strength. It’s also a contamination risk.~~ **Yep. A crack that goes through scatters the blue spectrum and weakens the cure. And it’s a contamination risk too.**”

Naseem set the light down, her shoulders dropping slightly. “So I can’t clean it and keep using it. Do I need to log this as a safety incident or just swap units?”

Leni pointed to the incident logbook sitting next to the autoclave controls. “~~Log it as equipment failure due to wear, not negligence, unless you dropped it. Swap the unit and tag this one for vendor analysis.~~ **Log it as equipment failure from wear, not negligence, unless you dropped it. Swap the unit and tag this one for the vendor.**”

Naseem frowned, tapping the pen again. “Vendor analysis costs us the warranty fee if they say impact damage.”

Leni’s gaze was steady. “~~With the ink test result, they’ll see structural fatigue. It protects you. Send it, but photograph the crack first for your record.~~ **With that ink result they’ll see structural fatigue, so you’re covered. Send it, but photograph the crack first.**”

Naseem exhaled, the tension leaving her jaw. She grabbed her phone. “Fine. One photo, then it goes in the biohazard bag for the vendor pickup. Thanks for the shortcut.”

Leni offered a small, professional smile as Naseem snapped the picture, the flash reflecting off the sterile steel surfaces. The light was dead, but the procedure held.

The vestment room smelled of cedar chips, dry starch, and the faint, sweet dust of old wool. Rows of robes hung on padded hangers, organized strictly by liturgical color: deep liturgical reds, somber purples, and bright, intimidating whites. The air was still and cool, protected from the drafty nave next door. Fahad stood before a heavy purple chasuble laid out on a large ironing board, a pair of sharp shears in one hand and a confused frown on his face. He was a grad student with a tight schedule and no patience for ambiguity; he needed to shorten the sleeves for a new choir member before rehearsal started in forty minutes. His mental state was frantic but contained, his eyes darting between the hem and the intricate gold braid that bordered the cuff.

Glenda entered quietly, carrying a thimble and a spool of silk thread. She was the parish seamstress, a woman who treated fabric with religious reverence. She was calm, methodical, and deeply knowledgeable about traditional garment construction. She wanted to ensure Fahad didn’t ruin the ceremonial trim, which was woven into the hem, not sewn on top. Her disposition was ~~warm but authoritative~~ **authoritative**; she saw the panic in Fahad’s posture and intended to diffuse it with clear, technical instruction. She enjoyed teaching, but only if the student respected the craft.

Fahad spoke first, his voice tight. “Glenda, I need to shorten these choir robe sleeves about three inches, but the gold trim is right at the edge. If I cut it, I ruin the whole robe.”

Glenda stepped closer, her fingers hovering over the fabric without touching it. “~~Let me see that. Ah, yes, this is a traditional woven border. You can’t cut through it because the trim isn’t attached; it’s the hem itself.~~ **Let me see. Mm, that’s a woven border. Don’t cut it. The trim’s not sewn on, it *is* the hem.**”

Fahad shifted his weight, the shears clicking against his palm. “So I’m stuck? The new soloist’s sleeves are dragging on the floor. We rehearse in twenty minutes.”

“~~Don’t panic.~~ **Oh, relax.**” Glenda turned the sleeve inside out, exposing the raw underside. “~~Look at the underside. See how the gold thread dips down into the fold? That’s your safety margin.~~ **See how the gold thread dips down into the fold? That’s your margin.**”

Fahad squinted, leaning in until his nose nearly touched the wool. “I see the fold, but there’s only half an inch of plain purple fabric inside before it hits the stitching. That’s not enough to let out.”

“~~You don’t need to let it out.~~ **You’re not letting anything out.**” She traced the invisible line with a crooked finger. “~~You need to unpick the top line of stitching, pull out three inches of the inner hem allowance, and resew it close to the edge.~~ **Unpick the top line of stitching, pull out three inches of the inner hem allowance, and sew it back down close to the edge.**”

He frowned, the shears lowering slightly. “Unpick the whole sleeve? That sounds like it’ll take an hour. Can’t I just fold it under and pin it?”

Glenda shook her head, her expression stern but gentle. “~~Pins will pop during swaying, and a permanent fold will create a bulky ridge against the arm. Picking the stitch takes five minutes if you use seam ripper carefully.~~ **Pins’ll pop out once they start swaying, and folding it under for good leaves a bulky ridge against the arm. Picking the stitch is five minutes if you’re careful with the seam ripper.**”

The clock on the wall ticked loudly. “Okay, five minutes works. Do I start from the cuff end or the shoulder?”

“~~Start at the cuff.~~ **Cuff end.**” She handed him a small, silver seam ripper. “~~Unpick only the top thread, leaving the inner fold intact. Pull the excess fabric from the inside, flatten it, and stitch the new hem line.~~ **Only the top thread, mind. Leave the inner fold alone. Then pull the extra fabric through from the inside, flatten it, and stitch your new hem line.**”

Fahad pocketed the shears and took the ripper, weighing its delicate handle. “Stitch by hand or machine? I don’t want a visible line on the outside.”

“~~Hand stitch.~~ **By hand.**” She unspooled a length of thread, holding it up to the light to match the shade. “~~Use a blind hem stitch with thread that matches the purple exactly. The gold trim hides the entry points if you aim high enough.~~ **Blind hem stitch, and get thread that’s the exact purple. Aim high and the gold trim hides where the needle goes in.**”

He nodded, feeling the tension in his shoulders ease. “Got it. Unpick, trim excess, blind stitch. Simple enough. Thanks for saving me from committing a liturgical fashion crime.”

Glenda smiled, a rare, crinkling thing around her eyes. “~~Glad to help. Just go slow on the ripper. The gold weave is fragile, and if you snag it, there’s no fixing that.~~ **Go slow on the ripper. That gold weave is fragile, and if you snag it, there’s no fixing it.**”

She watched him begin, her presence a steady anchor in the quiet room as the needle pierced the wool.

Alphonse clipped the tag to the frame, the plastic clacking softly. The wind picked up, whipping his jacket around his legs. He looked at the blocked-off climbing frame, then at the remaining list. There was no room for ambiguity now, only the cold, hard geometry of failure and repair.

They needed to define the acceptance criteria for the new validation protocol before their PI returned from lunch. They wanted concrete numbers, not philosophy. If Mathilde started talking about "process robustness" without metrics, Ari would cut them off.

Mathilde leaned against a nearby cart holding a rack of calibration standards. She wore a clean lab coat, sleeves rolled up, and held a tablet displaying the robot’s error logs. She enjoyed the puzzle of fine-tuning tolerances but knew Ari was ticking. Her goal was to provide the exact statistical thresholds Ari needed while ensuring they understood the safety margins. She was calm, precise, and ready to defend the "why" behind the numbers if pushed.

Ari spoke first, the pen clicking against their shirt sleeve. “I need the final acceptance criteria for the liquid handler validation by two. Just give me the pass/fail numbers for tip placement and volume precision.”

Mathilde swiped the tablet screen, bringing up a heatmap of the previous run’s errors. “~~Happy to. For tip placement, the industry standard is within fifty microns of the well center for ninety-nine percent of drops. That’s your geometric baseline.~~ **Sure. Tip placement, standard is within fifty microns of well center for ninety-nine percent of drops. That’s your baseline.**”

Ari frowned at the glowing red dots clustered near the plate’s perimeter. “Fifty microns? The well diameter is two hundred. That’s tight. Can we relax it to one hundred for the outer wells?”

“~~No, because edge wells suffer from XY axis backlash.~~ **No. Edge wells get XY axis backlash.**” Mathilde pointed to a jagged line on the graph. “~~If you relax the outer, you get carryover contamination. Keep the fifty-micron spec universal; it forces the calibration routine to run longer but saves you weeks of false positives later.~~ **Relax the outer ones and you get carryover contamination. Keep fifty microns everywhere. Calibration runs longer, but it saves you weeks of false positives later.**”

Ari sighed, uncrossing their arms. “Fine. Fifty microns, universal. What about the dispensing volume? I’m running nanoliter assays.”

Mathilde tapped the screen, shifting to a distribution curve. “~~For sub-microliter volumes, we look at Coefficient of Variation. Acceptable is under five percent for any given well. Total plate CV should be under eight percent.~~ **For sub-microliter volumes we go by CV. Under five percent for any given well, and under eight for the whole plate.**”

Ari rubbed their temple. “Eight percent total seems generous. My assay sensitivity drops if CV hits six.”

“~~It’s a buffer for pipette wear.~~ **That’s room for pipette wear.**” Mathilde gestured to the tip ejector station. “~~If you set the hard stop at five, you’ll be replacing tips every three runs. Eight gives you a safety margin for tip degradation over a full batch.~~ **Hard stop at five and you’re swapping tips every three runs. Eight covers tip degradation over a full batch.**”

“So if the plate reader shows seven-point-nine percent, I pass?”

“~~Yes, but log it as a warning.~~ **Yeah, but log it as a warning.**” Mathilde’s thumb hovered over the ‘print’ icon. “~~If it creeps to eight-one, halt and recalibrate the liquid level sensors.~~ **If it creeps to eight-one, stop and recalibrate the liquid level sensors.**”

Ari nodded, visualizing the workflow. “Okay. What defines a failed tip ejection? I saw one stick yesterday.”

“~~That’s a mechanical fault, not a precision metric.~~ **That’s a mechanical fault, not a precision thing.**” Mathilde looked down at the slightly misaligned ejection slot. “~~If any tip fails to eject in a batch of ninety-six, it’s an automatic fail. You can’t risk cross-contamination from a retained tip.~~ **One tip failing to eject in a batch of ninety-six is an automatic fail. A retained tip means cross-contamination, can’t risk that.**”

“Zero tolerance on ejections. Got it.” Ari checked their watch. “Does the robot need a self-test run before the actual sample batch?”

“~~Mandatory. Run the calibration protocol with empty tubes first. It takes four minutes. It verifies the suction sensors and the Z-axis height compensation.~~ **Yes, always. Run the calibration protocol with empty tubes first. Four minutes. It checks the suction sensors and the Z-axis height compensation.**”

Ari exhaled, tension bleeding from their shoulders. “Four minutes is fine. So: fifty microns placement, under five percent well CV, under eight plate CV, zero ejection failures.”

“~~Exactly.~~ **That’s it.**” Mathilde turned the tablet toward Ari, showing the ambient humidity graph. “~~And don’t forget to document the ambient temperature; humidity spikes can skew the liquid level sensing on this model.~~ **And write down the ambient temperature too. Humidity spikes can skew the liquid level sensing on this model.**”

“I’ll note the temp.” Ari stared at the robot, seeing it not as a broken tool but a calibrated instrument. “If I see any drift beyond that five-micron margin on the self-test, do I abort?”

“~~Abort and service.~~ **Abort and get it serviced.**” Mathilde capped a pen on her cart. “~~Better to lose four minutes now than ruin a week of samples. You’ve got the criteria, Ari.~~ **Better to lose four minutes now than a week of samples.**”

Ari pocketed the pen, the decision crystallizing. The numbers were hard, but they were theirs.

## B4180 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Sorcha

*10 edits applied, 0 rejected; 10.8% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The locker air hung heavy with the sharp sting of isopropyl alcohol and the metallic tang of dried acid. Naveed stood under the buzzing fluorescent tube, his foot tapping a restless rhythm against the workbench leg. In his palm, the 6S LiPo battery bulged grotesquely, its white shrink wrap stretched translucent and taut, resembling a bloated loaf rather than a power source. He needed a binary verdict, not a lecture, before the survey window closed.

Sorcha leaned against the steel shelving, tablet glowing in the dim light. Her eyes flicked from the inventory sheet to the distorted shape in Naveed’s hand, her posture remaining relaxed but alert.

Naveed held it up, his knuckles white. “Sorcha, look at this. Six-S pack swelled up overnight in the locker. Is it safe to charge it or do I bin it?”

She didn’t reach for it. “~~Don’t plug it in. Once a LiPo starts swelling, the internal structure is compromised. It’s a fire hazard now.~~ **Don’t plug it in. Once a LiPo swells, the insides are wrecked. It’s a fire risk now.**”

He glanced at the multimeter clamped to the terminals, where the digits held steady. “But the voltage reads 20.4 volts, right at storage level. Maybe it’s just gas pressure?”

Sorcha shook her head slightly, her tone flat. “~~That’s electrolyte breaking down. If you charge it, the heat builds faster than the skin can handle. Puncture is likely.~~ **No, that’s the electrolyte breaking down. Charge it and the heat builds faster than the skin can take. It’ll probably puncture.**”

Naveed sighed, rubbing his temple. The schedule was already fracturing. “I have a survey tomorrow morning. I only have one good pack left. Can I discharge it to zero?”

“~~Discharging a swollen cell is risky too. The internal resistance spikes, creating hot spots even when sitting still.~~ **Discharging a swollen one’s risky too. The internal resistance spikes and you get hot spots, even just sitting there.**”

He stared at the bulge, calculating the lost endurance. “So I’m flying on one battery? That cuts my endurance in half. This is bad timing.”

Sorcha stepped closer, pointing toward the red metal bucket mounted on the far wall. “~~Safety isn’t timing, Naveed. It’s physics. Put it in the metal bucket, not the trash can.~~ **Physics doesn’t care about your timing, Naveed. Metal bucket, not the trash can.**”

He grunted, lowering the battery toward the bench. “Fine. But why did this happen? I stored it at 3.8 volts per cell like we said.”

“~~Storage voltage is correct, but how long was it sitting? And was the locker humid?~~ **Storage voltage’s right. How long was it sitting, though? Was the locker humid?**”

“Two weeks. Humidity’s always high in this basement. Maybe the seals failed?”

“~~Seals degrade. Moisture gets in, reacts with the electrolyte, gas forms. Check the next pack’s shrink wrap for cracks.~~ **Seals wear out. Moisture gets in, reacts with the electrolyte, and you get gas. Look over the next pack’s shrink wrap for cracks.**”

Naveed picked up a roll of fire-safe tape, his movements slowing. “I’ll check the others. So, just bucket and tape? No slow discharge to save the cells?”

“~~No. The chemistry is dead. Tape the terminals, drop it in the quarantine bin, and order a replacement.~~ **No, it’s dead. Tape the terminals, drop it in the quarantine bin, order a replacement.**”

He nodded, the tension draining from his shoulders. “Understood. I’ll log the serial number now so I don’t forget to order the spare before tomorrow.”

Sorcha tapped her screen, printing a label. “~~Smart move. I’ll print the hazardous waste label for the bin while you’re at it.~~ **Good. I’ll print the hazardous waste label for the bin while you do that.**”

Naveed wrapped the terminals securely, the plastic crinkling loudly in the quiet room. “Thanks. I’ll grab the spare pack and start the pre-flight checklist on the drone itself.”

“~~Good idea.~~ **Yeah, do that.**” She paused, watching him move toward the shelves. “~~And Naveed? Always inspect the wrap before you power up, no matter the voltage reading.~~ **And Naveed? Check the wrap before you power up, whatever the voltage says.**”

He paused at the door, looking back at the red bucket. “Got it. Visual check first. I’m going to lose some flight time, but I’m glad I asked.”

## B4204 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Gita

*7 edits applied, 0 rejected; 5.8% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Xander leaned against the doorframe, the rough wood pressing into his lower back. The hallway light caught the dust motes dancing around his head, highlighting the dark circles under his eyes that no amount of cold coffee could erase. He hadn’t moved from this spot in ten minutes, rehearsing the apology in his head like a debug script, trying to strip it of all emotional subtext. The air in the kitchen was thick with the sharp, acrid scent of burnt garlic, a sensory reminder of Gita’s failed dinner attempt and the unpaid statements stacked on the counter behind her, their yellow highlights glaring like error codes.

“Hey, I realized I missed the electric bill split. The late fee hit. How do I fix this cleanly?”

Gita stood at the sink, the faucet running a steady, soft trickle. She held a ceramic plate under the stream, rinsing away the last traces of starch, her movements deliberate and unhurried. She didn’t turn around immediately, letting the sound of the water fill the silence for a beat before she spoke, her voice calm against the hum of the refrigerator.

“~~Cleanly is good. First, send the exact total plus the fee to me right now so we’re even.~~ **Fine by me. Just send me the total plus the fee now and we’re even.**”

Xander pulled his phone from his pocket, the screen’s blue light illuminating his stubble. He tapped rapidly, his thumb hovering over the send button with practiced precision, treating the transaction like a critical patch deployment.

“Done. Venmo’d you forty-two dollars. Is that the full amount with the penalty?”

Gita turned off the tap and set the plate on the drying rack, the ceramic clicking softly against the metal. She glanced at the stack of bills, the top one marked with aggressive yellow highlighter.

“~~Yes, that clears it. But if we keep doing manual checks every month, you’ll miss it again.~~ **Yep, that covers it. But if we keep checking by hand every month, you’ll miss it again.**”

Xander shifted his weight, the floorboards creaking under his boots. He rubbed the back of his neck, a gesture of genuine fatigue masking his defensive irritation. He wanted the issue closed, the debt settled, the social ledger balanced.

“I know. I’ve been swamped with the thesis defense prep. I just want to ensure it doesn’t happen again.”

Gita dried her hands on the dishtowel, the fabric rough against her palms. She looked at him, not with anger, but with the quiet assessment of someone observing a recurring bug in a complex system.

“~~Let’s automate it. We can set up a shared expense tracker that sends an invoice on the due date.~~ **So automate it. We set up a shared expense tracker that sends an invoice on the due date.**”

Xander frowned, the lines on his forehead deepening. The idea of another interface, another notification, another layer of management felt like added latency.

“Another app? That feels like more friction. I prefer just transferring when you ask.”

Gita leaned back against the counter, crossing her arms loosely. Her tone remained light, designed to lower his defenses rather than escalate them.

“~~That’s how we missed it last time. Human memory is unreliable; calendars are not. It takes five minutes to set up.~~ **That’s how we missed it last time. Memory’s lousy, calendars aren’t. Takes five minutes.**”

Xander sighed, the sound escaping him like steam from a valve. He conceded, recognizing the logic even if it chafed against his desire for immediate simplicity.

“Fine. Which one works best for splitting exact cents without rounding errors?”

Gita considered the options, weighing the convenience of familiarity against the precision of specialized tools.

“~~Splitwise handles fractions well, but a recurring split in Venmo works too if you don’t want a new login.~~ **Splitwise does fractions well, but a recurring split in Venmo works too if you don’t want another login.**”

Xander nodded, already opening the app. He preferred the familiar interface, the one he used daily. It was less cognitive load.

“Let’s stick to Venmo then. I’ll set a recurring transfer for my half on the first of the month.”

Gita offered a small, satisfied smile. It wasn’t about the money; it was about the structure, the removal of the variable human error.

“~~Perfect. Set it for the first, and I’ll pay the bill on the second. That gives you a buffer.~~ **Good. Set it for the first, I’ll pay the bill on the second. Gives you a buffer.**”

Xander watched the confirmation screen load, the digital receipt appearing like a resolved ticket. The tension in his shoulders dropped slightly.

“Deal. Recurring set for the first. Thanks for not making a bigger deal out of it.”

Gita picked up the dish towel again, folding it neatly. She gestured toward the living room with a tilt of her head.

“~~No problem. Just glad we have a system now. Go get some rest, you look wrecked.~~ **Eh, it’s fine. Go get some rest, you look wrecked.**”

Xander pushed off the doorframe, the movement stiff but less guarded. He walked out into the dim hallway, leaving the smell of burnt garlic and the yellow-highlighted bills behind in the clean, quiet kitchen.

## B4277 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Kimathi

*7 edits applied, 3 rejected; 6.9% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The corrugated walls of the irrigation shed hummed with the trapped heat of the morning sun, the air heavy with the scent of wet earth and stale motor oil. Vlada knelt in a shallow trench, the mud clinging to his jeans as he stared into the open valve box. The solenoid assembly vibrated with a violent, rhythmic shudder, sending tremors up the PVC pipe. He held his multimeter loosely, its leads dangling like dead vines, already convinced the electronics were lying to him.

Vlada spoke first. “This valve won’t stop shaking. I’ve got forty-eight volts going in, but the water is hammering instead of flowing. Am I looking at a dead solenoid or something else?”

Kimathi leaned against a stack of couplings, peeling a strip of Teflon tape from his pocket with methodical precision. He watched the valve’s convulsion with detached interest. “~~Solenoids usually fail by not opening, not by dancing. If it’s vibrating that hard, the diaphragm is fighting a pressure differential. Did you blow the lines clear before turning the water back on?~~ **Solenoids usually fail by not opening, not by dancing. Vibrating like that, the diaphragm’s fighting a pressure difference. You blow the lines clear before you turned the water back on?**”

Vlada wiped sweat from his forehead with the back of a muddy hand. “I did the standard winterization purge. Air went out, water came in. But now it sounds like a gravel mixer down there.”

“Air pockets trapped under the diaphragm cause chatter, sure, but not usually this violent.” Kimathi pushed off the stack, walking to the trench’s edge. “Let’s lift the bonnet. I bet you money the filter screen is choked with sand.”

Vlada hesitated, the screwdriver heavy in his grip. “I checked the screen yesterday. It looked clean. Do I really have to disassemble the whole head again? I have another zone to fix.”

“A clean-looking screen can still have grit lodged in the mesh pores.” Kimathi gestured to the exposed brass. “And if the spring is weak from sitting in freezing water, the seal won’t hold pressure. Lift it, I’ll watch.”

Vlada sighed, unscrewing the cap with frustrated twists. “Fine. Bonnet is off. Spring looks okay, tension feels normal. Screen is clear. There’s no debris, Kimathi. Just water and vibration.”

Kimathi crouched, squinting at the rubber components. “~~Look closer at the diaphragm seat. Is there a hairline crack or a nick where the metal stem might be scratching it? That’s common if you forced the manual override last spring.~~ **Look closer at the diaphragm seat. Any hairline crack, or a nick where the metal stem might’ve scratched it? Happens a lot if you forced the manual override last spring.**”

Vlada leaned in, squinting against the glare. “I don’t see a crack. The rubber looks intact. Could it be the water pressure upstream is too high for this valve size?”

“~~Unlikely. These are rated for eight zero PSI. If the pressure was too high, the valve would just blow the diaphragm off, not chatter.~~ **Nah. These are rated for eighty PSI. Too much pressure and it’d just blow the diaphragm off, not chatter.**” Kimathi traced the flow path with a finger. “Are you sure the inlet and outlet aren’t swapped?”

Vlada blinked, confused. “Swapped? That’s impossible. The arrow on the body points clearly downstream. I checked that twice.”

“Arrows get painted over, or old technicians ignore them.” Kimathi stood, brushing dirt from his knees. “If water flows backward, the diaphragm lifts but doesn’t seat right. It flutters. Turn off the main, swap the valve orientation, and test.”

Vlada stared at the valve, then at Kimathi’s calm face. “You think the previous guy installed it backward? That’s negligent. Okay, I’ll swap it. If it still chatters, I’m ordering a new assembly.”

“~~Swap it. If the chatter stops, you saved forty bucks and learned to trust the arrow less than the physics. Call me if the seal blows.~~ **Swap it. If the chatter stops, you just saved forty bucks and learned to trust the physics over the arrow. Call me if the seal blows.**”

Vlada reached up and killed the main line. The violent shaking ceased, leaving only the drip of residual water. He loosened the fittings, rotated the valve body one hundred and eighty degrees, and tightened them back down. “Swapped. Turning main on in three… two… one.”

He held his breath, listening. The pipe settled with a soft, steady rush. No hammering. No vibration.

Kimathi tilted his head, listening. “~~Sound it out for me. Is it smooth?~~ **Well? Sounds smooth?**”

Vlada exhaled, a tension releasing from his shoulders. “Silent. Water is shooting out even. You were right about the flow direction. The arrow was misleading.”

“~~Old paint job tricked you. Good catch.~~ **Old paint job fooled you. Nice catch.**” Kimathi tapped the valve casing gently. “~~Now tighten those bolts evenly so you don’t leak tomorrow.~~ **Tighten those bolts evenly now, or you’ll be leaking tomorrow.**”

Vlada nodded, gripping the wrench with renewed focus, the mud on his boots feeling less like a burden and more like part of the job.

## B4352 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Elise

*7 edits applied, 0 rejected; 10.1% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 3 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The generator yard smelled of burnt diesel and wet concrete, a thick, oily haze that clung to the back of Vartan’s throat. He stood on the cracked pad beneath the idling 500-kW unit, the vibration humming up through the soles of his boots. His tablet screen glowed with useless theoretical models, while the hairline fractures spreading from the anchor bolts mocked his thesis requirements. He adjusted his safety glasses, trying to look authoritative rather than lost.

Vartan spoke first, his voice barely rising above the mechanical clatter. “I’m looking at the hairline cracks under the 500-kW set. They radiate from the anchor bolts, but I don’t know if this is just thermal stress or the pad is actually failing.”

Elise stepped out from the shadow of the maintenance shack, wiping a grease-stained rag across her forearms. She moved with the easy confidence of someone who had memorized the yard’s every groan. She didn’t smile, but her posture relaxed as she approached the concrete slab. “~~Let’s rule out thermal expansion first. Did you measure the crack width at peak operating temperature versus ambient?~~ **Thermal first. Did you measure the crack width hot, at peak operating temperature, and cold?**”

Vartan tapped the screen of his tablet, then shrugged. “I don’t have a thermal history log for this specific unit. The previous crew just noted 'minor cracking' in the logbook three years ago.”

She reached into her tool belt and produced a small, hooked plastic pry bar. “~~Then we check for movement. Take this plastic pry bar and gently test the bond around the bolt base. If it feels loose or you hear a hollow tap, you have delamination.~~ **Then check for movement. Take this plastic pry bar and go easy around the bolt base. Loose or hollow-sounding, that’s delamination.**”

Vartan knelt, the concrete cold against his knees. He slid the tool under the bolt washer, pressing lightly. It didn’t give. “It feels solid, but there’s a fine powder dusting the crack edges. Is that normal weathering or spalling?”

Elise leaned in, squinting at the gray dust. She pulled a digital crack width gauge from her pocket, its metallic jaws gleaming dully. “~~That powder is likely cement paste from surface erosion. Use the digital gauge to measure the widest point. If it’s under one-sixteenth of an inch, it’s usually cosmetic.~~ **That’s just cement paste, surface erosion mostly. Put the digital gauge on the widest point. Under one-sixteenth of an inch, it’s usually cosmetic.**”

Vartan clamped the gauge around the fracture. The digital readout flickered, then settled. “It reads four thousandths of an inch. That’s well within cosmetic range, but the crack runs toward the drainage trench.”

Elise pointed a calloused finger toward the narrow channel running alongside the pad. “~~Then inspect the trench for blockage. If water pools under the pad during heavy rain, it can wash out the sub-base, creating voids that eventually collapse the concrete.~~ **Then look at the trench for blockage. Water pooling under the pad in heavy rain can wash out the sub-base, and the voids eventually collapse the concrete.**”

Vartan frowned, recalling his earlier inspection. “I checked the trench yesterday; it’s clear. So if it’s not water damage and not thermal, what’s causing the radiation pattern?”

Elise straightened up, brushing concrete dust from her coveralls. “~~Likely plastic shrinkage from the original pour. If the concrete cured too fast in high heat, it pulled away from the rebar. As long as it’s not widening, it’s safe.~~ **Probably plastic shrinkage from the original pour. If the concrete cured too fast in high heat, it pulled away from the rebar. It’s safe as long as it’s not widening.**”

Relief washed over Vartan, easing the tension in his shoulders. He capped his pen. “Okay, so I document the width, confirm the trench is clear, and note it as historical shrinkage?”

“~~Exactly.~~ **Yep.**” Elise nodded, turning back toward the shack. “~~Take a photo with a ruler for scale, seal the crack with a flexible epoxy if you want, and move on. It’s not a structural threat.~~ **Get a photo with a ruler in it for scale. Seal the crack with a flexible epoxy if you want, then move on. It’s not structural.**”

Vartan snapped the photo, the flash momentarily blinding in the gloom of the yard. The engine roared on, steady and unbothered, while the data finally made sense.

The arrivals hall smelled of recycled air, floor wax, and damp wool. Carousel Three groaned, spinning its lazy metal circle while travelers slumped against the railing. Beren stood by the glass partition of the Baggage Service Office, his black roller bag tilted on three wheels. The fourth wheel housing hung loose, flapping like a severed tongue. He checked his watch. Ninety minutes to the lab meeting. The broken bag was an unacceptable variable in his equation.

Ksenia sat behind the glass, headset resting around her neck like a silver collar. Her fingers moved across the keyboard with rhythmic precision, de-escalating the morning’s frustrations one click at a time. She saw Beren’s tight jaw and the way he tapped his foot. He needed speed, not sympathy.

Beren leaned in, his breath fogging the glass slightly. “Excuse me. I just found this damage on my bag from the flight. I need to file a report and ideally get a replacement or repair authorization before my next connection.”

Ksenia paused, turning her chair slightly. She studied the bag’s exposed plastic ribs, then looked back at Beren. Her voice was calm, measured. “~~I’m sorry to hear that. Let’s take a look. Was the damage visible when you picked up the bag at the carousel, or did you notice it later?~~ **Ah, that’s rough. Let me see. Did you spot it right at the carousel, or later?**”

“Visible right away. The wheel housing is torn off and there’s a crack in the shell.” He shifted his weight, impatient for the next step. “I’m on a tight schedule, so how quickly can we process this?”

“~~We need to log it as a Property Irregularity Report first.~~ **First thing, I log it as a Property Irregularity Report.**” She gestured to the scanner. “~~I’ll scan your boarding pass and bag tag. This ensures the airline accepts liability for the incident.~~ **I’ll scan your boarding pass and bag tag. That’s what gets the airline to own it.**”

Beren produced the crumpled papers from his jacket pocket. “I have them. Do I need to fill out a separate physical form, or is the digital scan enough for the initial claim?”

“~~The digital entry is mandatory.~~ **The digital entry’s the one that counts.**” She held out her hand. “~~I’ll create the PIR now. While I do that, can you confirm the bag tag is still attached to the handle?~~ **I’ll make the PIR now. Is the bag tag still on the handle?**”

“Yes, it’s still on. I didn’t remove it because I assumed you’d need it.” He glanced at the clock on the wall again. “I have a meeting in ninety minutes.”

“~~Understood.~~ **Got it.**” She typed rapidly, the clack-clack-clack syncing with her breath. “~~I’m entering the serial number now. For this type of damage, we can offer an immediate interim repair loaner if you have a layover.~~ **Putting the serial number in now. For damage like this, we can give you a loaner while it’s repaired, if you’ve got a layover.**”

Beren frowned, processing the jargon. “Loaner? Is that a full suitcase or just a repair kit? I can’t travel with broken wheels.”

“~~It’s a complete temporary bag from our inventory. You’d return it within four weeks after we finalize the compensation claim.~~ **No, it’s a whole bag, off our shelf. You bring it back within four weeks, once the compensation claim’s sorted.**”

He exhaled, the tension in his shoulders dropping a fraction. “That sounds workable. Where is the loaner desk, and do I need another ticket for that transaction?”

“No additional ticket needed. I’ll mark this claim as “Urgent Repair” in the system, which flags the loaner desk to prepare a bag for you immediately.”

“Great. Can you print a copy of the PIR reference number for my records? I’ll need it for the insurance filing later.”

The printer whirred, spitting a crisp white slip. “~~Absolutely. Here is your receipt with the case number. Please proceed to the counter to the left; they have your loaner ready once I update the status.~~ **Sure, here’s your receipt with the case number. Go to the counter on your left. Your loaner’ll be ready once I update the status.**”

Beren took the receipt, folding it neatly into his wallet. “Thanks. I appreciate the quick turnaround. I’ll head to the left now.”

Ksenia offered a small, professional nod. “~~You’re welcome. Safe travels for the rest of your trip, and we’ll be in touch regarding the final resolution soon.~~ **No problem. Safe trip. We’ll be in touch about the final resolution soon.**”

Beren turned, the broken wheel scraping loudly on the tile, and walked toward the loaner desk. Ksenia watched him go, then typed the final status update, closing the file with a satisfied click.

The factory air hung heavy with the sharp tang of curing sealant and hot steel, a thick blanket that made every breath feel laborious. Sunlight sliced through the high clerestory windows, catching dust motes that swirled violently above the oil-stained concrete floor. Rheaia stood on the rolling scaffold, her knuckles white as she gripped a thermal camera in one hand and a clipboard in the other. Below her, the modular classroom prototype lay exposed, a skeletal frame draped in white insulation and silver vapor barriers that shimmered like fish scales. She adjusted her stance, leaning into the corner joint where the floor panel met the wall module, her posture rigid with the kind of academic anxiety that tightened the chest.

Amosel approached, his boots crunching softly on the scattered debris. He carried a laser thermometer like a baton, his demeanor calm amidst the deafening hum of distant pressurized air systems. He viewed the chaotic scene not as a puzzle to be solved by brute force, but as a system waiting to be read.

Rheaia turned to him, her eyes narrowed against the glare. “Amosel, the thermal camera readings at these module joints are all over the map. Am I misreading the emissivity or is the prototype just leaking heat everywhere?”

Amosel stopped beside the scaffold, tilting his head to examine the reflective surface. “~~The vapor barrier is the problem, not your technique. It’s too reflective for standard settings. You need to bump the emissivity down to zero-point-eight-five to get a true surface temp.~~ **It’s the vapor barrier, not you. It’s too shiny for the default settings. Drop the emissivity to zero-point-eight-five and you’ll get a true surface temp.**”

Rheaia fumbled with the camera’s menu, her thumb slipping on the glass screen. “I tried that, but the steel studs still read twenty degrees warmer than the insulated panels. Is that a defect or just physics?”

Amosel raised the laser thermometer, aiming it casually at a exposed stud. “~~That’s a thermal bridge, not a defect. Steel conducts heat four hundred times better than your insulation. It’s pulling energy straight through the connection.~~ **That’s a thermal bridge, not a defect. Steel conducts heat four hundred times better than your insulation, so it’s pulling energy straight through the connection.**”

She looked from the camera’s display to the cold, grey steel, frustration tightening her jaw. “But the spec says the U-value should be uniform. If this bridge is real, I fail the energy model. Can we seal it?”

“~~You can’t seal a conductive path. You have to break the continuity. Are you using thermal breaks at the floor-to-wall connection?~~ **Can’t seal a conductive path, you’ve got to break it. Are there thermal breaks at the floor-to-wall connection?**”

Rheaia scanned the blueprints on her clipboard, her brow furrowing. “The drawings show rigid foam inserts, but the installers skipped them on Bay Three. I didn’t catch it until now.”

Amosel nodded, a small, knowing smile touching his lips. “~~That’s your culprit. The foam was supposed to isolate the steel plates. Without it, you have a direct metal-to-metal heat highway.~~ **There’s your culprit. That foam’s meant to isolate the steel plates. Without it, it’s a metal-to-metal heat highway.**”

She exhaled sharply, the sound lost in the factory’s roar. “So I need to retrofit foam inserts? That’s a massive rework for a prototype.”

“~~For the thesis, document the delta. Measure the surface temp with the laser on the steel versus the adjacent wall to quantify the loss.~~ **For the thesis, just document the delta. Hit the steel with the laser, then the wall next to it, and compare.**”

Rheaia lowered the thermal camera and picked up the laser, its red dot dancing across the metal. “Laser is faster. If I log the difference at every joint, does that satisfy the evaluation criteria?”

“~~It proves the bridge exists. Calculate the added heat loss per square meter, and you’ll have the data your advisor needs to justify the rework cost.~~ **It proves the bridge is there. Work out the added heat loss per square meter and your advisor has what they need to justify the rework cost.**”

She nodded, the tension in her shoulders easing slightly as the path forward became clear, concrete and measurable. The dust continued to swirl, indifferent to her impending relief.

## B4791 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Rina

*0 edits applied, 3 rejected; 0.0% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The late afternoon sun cut through the corrugated metal lean-to, illuminating dust motes dancing above the workbench. The air smelled of sawdust and the faint, ammonia bite from the nearby coop, where hens were already clucking their evening settlement. Maddox stood before the half-finished prototype, his arms crossed tight against his chest, a foot tapping an impatient rhythm on the concrete floor. The Raspberry Pi in its waterproof box looked fragile next to the heavy-duty linear actuator.

Maddox: The actuator jams if the wind hits the door hard, and my script just keeps sending voltage until the motor burns. I need a fail-safe that actually stops the system.

Rina leaned against the doorframe, her tea mug warm in her hands. She watched the shadow of the coop door stretch long across the bench.

Rina: Burned motors are the classic rookie mistake. You need two layers: one to detect the stall electrically, and one to ensure the door can be opened by hand if power dies.

Maddox ran a hand through his hair, eyes fixed on the tangle of wires. Maddox: Electrical first. I have a current sensor, but adding one complicates the wiring mess inside this tiny project box. Is there a simpler way?

Rina took a slow sip, setting the mug down on a stack of manuals. Rina: Use a timeout counter in your code. If the door doesn’t reach the 'open' or 'closed' switch within ten seconds, cut power immediately. It’s free and requires no extra hardware.

Maddox frowned, the logic seeming too thin for the physical reality of wind and rust. Maddox: That sounds fragile. What if the sensor is dirty and never triggers, even if the door is open?

Rina reached out, tapping the galvanized steel frame lightly. Rina: Then you add a second, cheaper sensor at the top. A magnetic reed switch is passive, waterproof, and impossible to dirty. If the bottom says open and the top says closed after ten seconds, kill the motor.

Maddox considered the image of a dead board and locked birds. Maddox: Two switches. Fine. But if the Pi bricks or the battery dies, the door stays latched. How do I get the chickens in during a blackout?

Rina didn’t hesitate. Rina: You don’t rely on electronics for that. You install a manual release lever on the exterior. A simple cam lock that disengages the gear train when pulled.

Maddox shook his head, already picturing the rust and the kids from next door. Maddox: I hate external mechanisms. Rain, rust, kids playing with it. It adds maintenance.

Rina’s expression hardened slightly, her tone dropping the warmth for a moment of stark utility. Rina: It’s a ten-dollar lever, Maddox. It’s not a feature; it’s a life-safety requirement for animals. You’d never trust a single software line to keep your car doors open, would you?

The silence stretched, filled only by the distant cooing. Maddox uncrossed his arms, shoulders dropping. Maddox: Point taken. So, reed switch for verification, timeout to stop the burn, and a manual override for the power-loss scenario.

Rina nodded, picking up a marker from the bench. Rina: Exactly. And put a thermal fuse on the motor wire as a last-ditch hardware backup. If the timeout fails, the heat opens the circuit. Triple redundancy, minimal cost.

Maddox picked up the schematic, tracing the new path with his finger. Maddox: Thermal fuse is a good idea. I’ll buy the reed switch and find a cam lock. Can you show me how to mount the lever so it doesn’t interfere with the seal?

Rina stepped closer, pointing to a gap in the frame. Rina: Sure. We’ll drill a small access port and use a cable pull. It keeps the weather out and the mechanism simple. Grab the drill, and let’s look at the frame geometry.

Maddox moved to the tool rack, the frustration replaced by a focused calm. Maddox: Thanks, Rina. I appreciate you not just telling me to rewrite the whole library. I’ll have the parts ordered by tonight.

Rina watched him go, a small smile touching her lips. Rina: Happy to help. Just remember: the code is the brain, but the hardware is the muscle. Protect the muscle, and the brain doesn’t matter as much. See you tomorrow.

The workshop settled back into its quiet hum, the golden light fading as the first stars appeared over the barn roof.

## B4807 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Abdul

*6 edits applied, 0 rejected; 7.4% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 1 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Selene stood beside the bright red adaptive chair, its right brake lever hanging loose like a broken finger. The workshop air hung heavy with the scent of ozone and fresh foam, the fluorescent hum vibrating in her teeth. She gripped the socket wrench, knuckles white, staring at the axle nut that refused to yield smooth operation. Two hours until the demo.

“Abdul, the right brake on this ultralight is sticking. I tightened the axle nut, but the lever drags and won’t spring back. What’s the sequence to free it without stripping the threads?”

Abdul did not look up from the calipers in his hands. He rubbed the microfiber cloth over the steel jaws, a rhythmic, soothing motion. The chrome rims of three other chairs lined the back wall, silent witnesses to her panic.

“~~Don’t touch the axle nut yet—that just pushes the hub in. First, check the brake arm pivot. Is the bushing dry or gummed up with old grease?~~ **Leave the axle nut alone, that just pushes the hub in. Look at the brake arm pivot first. That bushing dry, or gunked up with old grease?**”

Selene leaned in, squinting at the dark crevice where the arm met the frame. “Looks clean. I think the tension spring is compressed too far. Should I loosen the locknut on the arm?”

Abdul finally lifted his eyes, wiping his palms on his trousers. “~~Yes, but only one turn counter-clockwise. Watch the arm; if it swings out too wide, the pads won’t touch the rim. You want about two millimeters of clearance.~~ **Yeah, but just one turn counter-clockwise. Watch the arm. If it swings out too wide, the pads won’t touch the rim. You want about two millimeters of clearance.**”

She adjusted the wrench, feeling the metal shift. “Two millimeters sounds tiny. If I loosen it further, will it fix the stickiness?”

“~~No, looser means less grip.~~ **Nope, looser just means less grip.**” Abdul stepped closer, pointing to the thin steel wire running along the frame. “Stickiness usually means the caliper is misaligned or the wire is kinked. Check the control cable inside the housing first.”

Selene pulled the cable gently. It slid without resistance. She turned her attention to the star-shaped nut nestled against the brake arm. “Cable pulls smooth. I’m looking at the star locknut now. Do I tighten it to eliminate play, or leave a fraction of a turn loose?”

“~~Tighten it until you feel firm resistance, then back off a quarter turn. You need just enough friction to hold position, but enough give to prevent binding.~~ **Snug it till it pushes back, then back off a quarter turn. You want it holding position but not binding.**”

Selene hesitated, the wrench hovering. The instinct to clamp down was strong, born of years of overtightening bicycle seats. “Backing off feels wrong. It feels loose. If I back off, won’t the brake vibrate off during rolling?”

“~~Not if the tension is even on both sides.~~ **Not if it’s even on both sides.**” Abdul watched her face, waiting for the doubt to settle. “Squeeze the lever now. Does it snap back cleanly?”

She squeezed. The lever returned, but sluggishly. “It’s better, but still hesitant. I’m going to crank the adjustment screw in to force engagement.”

“Stop.” Abdul’s voice dropped, flat and final. He reached out, stopping her hand. “Cranking the screw compresses the pads permanently, causing that drag. Reset the screw to the halfway point and align the caliper square to the rim.”

Selene let out a breath she hadn’t realized she was holding. She adjusted the screw, squinting to ensure the pads faced the wheel squarely. “Fine. Aligned. Squeezing lever... okay, it’s crisp now. No drag on release.”

The *thwack* was sharp, satisfying. Abdul nodded, a rare small smile touching his lips. “That’s the sweet spot. Mark the locknut position with a paint pen so you know the baseline for future checks.”

Selene fetched the pen, drawing a bright red line across the nut. “Marked. Thanks, Abdul. I thought I needed to force it, but the alignment was the issue all along.”

“~~Always check alignment before tension. You’re good to go for the demo. Double-check the left side just to be safe.~~ **Alignment first, then tension. You’re set for the demo. Give the left side a look too, though.**”

Selene set the wrench down, the tension in her shoulders dissolving into the smell of hot metal. The chair sat ready, balanced, and silent.

## B5141 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Munir

*7 edits applied, 0 rejected; 11.1% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Arlette tapped her pen against the rim of her cold coffee cup, the sharp *click* echoing too loudly in the soundproofed corner of the lab. The air was stagnant, scrubbed clean of the usual hallway hum, leaving only the sterile glare of the LED lamp and the gray, egg-crate foam walls. She checked her watch again. Twelve minutes until office hours. She needed a definitive answer for her thesis hardware budget, not vague adjectives.

Munir sat across from her, calm and ready. He had organized the two headsets—bulky Logitech on the left, sleek Jabra on the right—and positioned a small digital decibel meter between them. His fingers rested lightly on the tablet, spec sheets loaded and waiting. He preferred the binary nature of decibels to the subjective rants of most students.

Arlette spoke first, her voice tight with impatience. “I need to pick a headset for the renovation pilot. I have thirty models on the list, but the spec sheets are just marketing noise. How do I actually compare their noise reduction for the callers?”

Munir pointed a stylus at the tablet screen, highlighting a column of numbers. “~~Ignore the marketing blurbs. Look for the SNR Improvement rating in decibels. That number tells you how much background noise the mic rejects while keeping your voice clear.~~ **Skip the blurbs. Find the SNR Improvement rating, it’s in decibels. It’s how much background noise the mic kills while your voice still comes through clean.**”

Arlette narrowed her eyes, trying to visualize the scale. “So if Headset A says 30dB and Headset B says 40dB, B is twice as good?”

Munir nodded once, precise. “~~Logarithmically, yes. A 10dB jump means the background noise floor drops by a factor of ten. That is significant for an open office environment.~~ **Sort of, it’s logarithmic. Every 10dB jump drops the background noise floor by a factor of ten. In an open office, that’s a big deal.**”

She scribbled the math on her notepad, the pen scratching aggressively. “That seems straightforward. Why does every review site talk about ANC then? Does that help the mic?”

Munir shook his head slowly, separating the concepts with a gesture. “~~ANC is for your ears, not the mic. It uses speakers to cancel noise for the listener. For the caller, you need beamforming mics and DSP algorithms. Don’t confuse input and output quality.~~ **ANC’s for your ears, not the mic. It uses the speakers to cancel noise for the listener. For the caller, you want beamforming mics and DSP algorithms. Don’t mix up input and output.**”

Arlette picked up the Jabra headset, feeling its lightweight plastic. “Okay, input quality. I have the Logitech and the Jabra units here. The Jabra claims better mic rejection. Can we test that live?”

Munir turned his chair slightly to face the speaker system behind Arlette. “~~Sure. Put the decibel meter in the center. I will play traffic noise from the speaker behind you. You speak a standard sentence into the mic.~~ **Sure. Set the meter in the middle. I’ll run traffic noise from the speaker behind you, and you just say a normal sentence into the mic.**”

Arlette donned the Jabra, the foam cup sealing over her ear. A roar of simulated city traffic blasted from the rear speaker, vibrating the desk. She leaned into the boom mic. “Noise is loud. I’m speaking now. ‘Testing one, two, three.’ Did that work?”

Munir watched the waveform on the monitor, his expression neutral but focused. “~~Listening to the monitor feed... the Jabra dropped the noise floor significantly. The Logitech let the traffic hiss bleed through. The Jabra wins on raw rejection.~~ **Okay, on the monitor the Jabra cut the noise floor way down. The Logitech let the traffic hiss bleed through. Jabra wins on rejection, no contest.**”

Arlette pulled the headset off, her voice clear in the sudden quiet. “What about latency? The Jabra is wireless. I need real-time interaction for the support agents.”

Munir scrolled down the spec sheet, stopping at the connection details. “~~Check the Bluetooth codec specs. If it uses aptX Low Latency, you are looking at under 40 milliseconds. That is imperceptible to humans.~~ **Look at the Bluetooth codec. If it’s aptX Low Latency, you’re under 40 milliseconds. Nobody can hear that.**”

Arlette capped her pen. “40 milliseconds is fine. The Jabra it is. Thanks for the shortcut, Munir.”

Munir began packing away the decibel meter, his movements efficient. “~~Anytime. Just remember to update the firmware before deployment; older versions have bugs with the noise gate.~~ **No problem. Update the firmware before you roll them out, though. The older versions have noise gate bugs.**”

Arlette gathered her notes, the decision made. The sterile silence of the lab felt less oppressive now, filled instead with the quiet satisfaction of a problem solved. She turned toward the door, checking her watch with a faint smile.

The freezer aisle hummed with a low, mechanical indifference, its glass doors exhaling plumes of frost that curled into the stagnant, supercooled air. Niamh stood frozen in the center of the aisle, her basket containing only milk and eggs. The fluorescent lights buzzed with a headache-inducing frequency, and the floor felt slick under her soles. Her brain was fried; twelve hours of lab data mining had drained every ounce of cognitive load she possessed. She stared blankly at rows of identical plastic containers, paralyzed by the sheer volume of choices that demanded more mental energy than she had left to give.

Xiao stopped a few feet away, his own basket held with distinct technical precision: two frozen pizzas, a bag of broccoli, a single bottle of wine. He observed Niamh’s paralyzed stance, recognizing the familiar slump of decision fatigue. He viewed meal prep as an optimization problem, ready to deploy his internal database of grocery efficiency hacks.

Niamh spoke first, her voice flat with exhaustion. “I have four days of coding ahead and zero energy for chopping. What’s the most efficient freezer dinner strategy that doesn’t taste like cardboard?”

“~~Efficiency means high protein, low prep time. Skip the stir-fry kits; the sauce packets are sodium bombs and the veggies are mush. Grab the vacuum-sealed salmon fillets instead.~~ **High protein, low prep. Skip the stir-fry kits, the sauce packets are sodium bombs and the veggies are mush. Get the vacuum-sealed salmon fillets.**”

She glanced at the generic chicken kits, then back at the expensive blue boxes. “Salmon is expensive. My budget is tight until the stipend hits. Can’t we just do the chicken kits?”

“~~You can, but the yield is poor. You pay for three dollars of sauce and get six ounces of meat. Salmon is five dollars but twelve ounces of actual food. Better ROI.~~ **You can, but you don’t get much. Three dollars of sauce for six ounces of meat. Salmon’s five dollars for twelve ounces of actual food. Better deal.**”

“ROI on calories, sure. But what about the cooking time? I’m talking microwave or ten minutes max.”

“~~Microwave kills the texture, making it rubbery. Ten minutes in the oven or air fryer is fine. If you’re that tired, buy the pre-marinated ones.~~ **Microwave makes it rubbery. Ten minutes in the oven or air fryer is fine. If you’re that tired, just buy the pre-marinated ones.**”

“Pre-marinated usually means preservatives. I’m trying to eat clean, or at least cleaner. Is there a middle ground?”

“~~Buy the plain fillets and a jar of good mustard or teriyaki sauce. Two ingredients, zero chopping, ready in eight minutes. It’s a scalable system.~~ **Buy the plain fillets and a jar of good mustard or teriyaki sauce. Two ingredients, zero chopping, ready in eight minutes.**”

She considered the math, nodding slowly. “Okay, plain salmon plus condiments. But I need carbs. Do I freeze the rice or buy it bagged?”

“~~Bagged steamable rice pouches. They’re lightweight, don’t freeze solid, and heat in two minutes. Pair that with frozen spinach—just drop a handful in.~~ **Bagged steamable rice pouches. They don’t freeze solid and they heat in two minutes. Throw in a handful of frozen spinach with it.**”

“Spinach shrinks to nothing. How many bags do I need to buy to actually feel full?”

“~~Two bags per batch. The volume is air, so double what your eyes tell you. Store it in a ziplock to save freezer space.~~ **Two bags per batch. It’s mostly air, so double what your eyes tell you. Keep it in a ziplock, saves freezer space.**”

Niamh exhaled, the tension in her shoulders easing slightly. “Got it. Plain salmon, steam rice, bulk spinach, one jar sauce. Write that down for me or I’ll forget by the checkout.”

“~~Done. I texted the list to your phone. Now grab a protein powder; your post-lab snacks are going to be critical.~~ **Done, just texted it to you. Oh, and grab a protein powder. You’ll want it for post-lab snacks.**”

The scanner beeped somewhere down the aisle. Niamh adjusted her grip on the basket, the chaos of the freezer aisle suddenly reduced to a manageable, executable script.

## B5200 · assistant-like-baseline · helper Ezequiel

*9 edits applied, 0 rejected; 15.2% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

Dominika sat cross-legged on the linoleum, her stained apron crinkling as she shifted the weight of her sketchbook. The fluorescent tubes above buzzed in a rhythm that matched the throbbing behind her eyes. She stared at the 1924 zoning map, its edges yellowed and brittle.

“I have a stack of 1924 zoning maps here, and I want to collé them. How do I do this without the archive or the historical society calling me a vandal?”

Ezequiel leaned against the steel table, wiping a smudge of graphite from his knuckles. He looked at the map, then at her tight jaw.

“~~You don’t hide the provenance, you highlight it. The key is that you are not republishing the map as a reference tool, but using it as raw texture.~~ **Don’t hide where it came from, show it off. You’re not republishing the map as a reference tool, you’re just using it as texture.**”

She tapped a finger on the crisp street grid. “Texture is vague. If I leave the street names visible, isn’t that still reproducing the official record?”

He pushed off the table, stepping closer to examine the faded ink. “~~Only if you claim it is accurate. By layering it with other materials, you break its function as a utility. The moment you cover a corner with paint, it becomes art, not data.~~ **Only if you claim it’s accurate. Layer it with other stuff and it stops working as a utility. Cover a corner with paint and it’s art, not data.**”

Dominika shook her head, glancing at the handwritten annotations in the margins—scribbles of old addresses. “I don’t want to cover it. I like the clarity of the grid. But the handwritten notes in the margins worry me.”

Ezequiel smiled faintly. “~~Those are ghost traces of bureaucracy. They add to the narrative of time passing. Keep them, but do not let them dominate the composition.~~ **Those are ghosts of bureaucracy. They add to the sense of time passing. Keep them, just don’t let them take over the composition.**”

She traced a finger near, but not touching, the blue ink. “So I’m allowed to use the ink, but not the intent? That feels like a thin line.”

“~~It is the only line that exists. Respect the object’s fragility, acknowledge its source in your artist statement, and never sell the piece as a historical document.~~ **It’s the only line there is. Go easy on the paper, name the source in your artist statement, and never sell the piece as a historical document.**”

She sighed, pulling a pencil from her ear. “My statement is usually two sentences. Do I need a disclaimer?”

“~~A credit line is enough. 'Materials sourced from Municipal Archive, Collection X.' That protects you and honors them.~~ **A credit line’s enough. ’Materials sourced from Municipal Archive, Collection X.’ Covers you, and it’s fair to them.**”

Dominika eyed the small bottle of adhesive in her jar. “And the adhesive? The catalog says no modern glues near 1920s paper.”

“~~Use methylcellulose, not wheat paste. It is reversible and pH neutral. Test a scrap corner first, always.~~ **Use methylcellulose, not wheat paste. It’s reversible and pH neutral. And test it on a scrap corner first, always.**”

She frowned, considering the permanence of art. “Reversible? If it’s art, why does it need to be reversible?”

“~~Because ethical use means you never damage the original beyond repair. If you slip up, a conservator can lift the layer. That is the safety net.~~ **Because you never want to wreck the original for good. If you slip up, a conservator can lift the layer. That’s your safety net.**”

Dominika uncapped her pen, writing the points on a sticky note. “Okay. Methylcellulose, credit line, layering to break utility. I can write that down.”

Ezequiel watched the ink dry. “~~You can also write that on a sticky note. It helps remember that the map is a guest in your work, not the host.~~ **Stick the map’s place on there too. It’s a guest in your work, not the host.**”

She stuck the note to her sketchbook. “Noted. I’ll treat the ink like a conversation partner, not a spreadsheet. Thanks, Ezequiel.”

He picked up his rag and nodded toward the door. “~~Anytime. Just don’t use the blue ink notes for a real estate listing, and you’re fine.~~ **Sure. Just don’t use the blue ink notes for a real estate listing and you’re fine.**”
