“Margot, do you have a minute before I head out? I need your take on this Vienna offer.”

She slid her phone into her pocket, the motion smooth and final. “I’m grabbing my coat; if it’s quick, but I have to leave.”

Jin stepped closer, the floorboards creaking under his weight. He held the letter out, a shield and a weapon. “It’s a conservatory spot. They want me to commit by Friday. Should I take it?”

Margot didn’t look at the paper. She adjusted the strap of her bag, eyes fixed on the exit door. “~~That’s a big decision; you really should talk to your advisor first.~~ **That’s a big one. Talk to your advisor first.**”

“I’ve already called them. They’re useless. I need a peer perspective, not bureaucracy.” His voice was tight, stripping away the politeness.

“~~I don’t know Vienna; maybe ask someone who’s actually been there.~~ **I don’t know Vienna. Ask someone who’s actually been there.**” She shrugged, pulling her coat from the back of a chair.

“You’re the only one here who understands the pressure. Just say yes or no.” He waited, the silence stretching between them, filled only by the distant hum of the HVAC system.

“~~I can’t make that for you; it’s your career path.~~ **I can’t call that for you. It’s your career.**” Margot slipped her arms into the sleeves, zipping the coat up to her chin.

“Don’t give me platitudes. Does the risk pay off for a cellist my age?” Jin’s gaze dropped to the stacks of chair backs lining the walls, empty seats waiting for an ensemble that wasn’t there.

“~~Risk is subjective; I’d check the alumni placement stats online.~~ **Depends who you ask. I’d look up the alumni placement stats online.**” She turned her body toward the door, signaling the end of the conversation.

“I have the stats. They’re vague. I need your gut instinct, not data.” He took a step forward, pleading with the silence.

“My gut says I’m late for dinner, so you’ll have to figure it out.” Her tone was flat, observational, devoid of the weight he sought.

“You’re avoiding the question because you don’t want to be responsible.” Jin lowered the letter, his shoulders slumping slightly.

“Exactly; I’m not your career counselor, Jin.” She reached for the door handle, the brass cold and solid.

“Fine. I’ll guess. Thanks for nothing.” He turned back to the piano, the purple light reflecting off the closed lid.

“~~Good luck with the guess; have a safe drive.~~ **Good luck with the guess. See you.**” Margot pushed the door open, the sound of the street bleeding in for a second before it clicked shut, leaving Jin alone in the cavern of shadowed wood.

The room was a windowless box, air thick and blue with sandalwood incense. A single ceramic bowl sat on the low table, emitting steady plumes of white-gray smoke that twisted in the stillness. The light was dim, filtered through a haze that made the charcoal paper on Kaori’s lap look like wet slate. She leaned forward, charcoal stick poised, eyes narrowed against the smoke, desperate to capture the way the vapor dissolved into the background. Leonid sat cross-legged on a cushion three feet away, staring blankly at a wall. Headphones rested around his neck; his thumb scrolled rhythmically across his phone screen, counting down the minutes until he could leave.

“Leonid, I’m struggling to get the translucency right on the smoke,” Kaori said, her voice hushed by the heavy air. “How do you keep it airy?”

Leonid didn’t look up from his screen. “I don’t really do smoke drawings; I think Professor Hane covers atmospheric effects in his section.”

Kaori tapped the charcoal against her knee, a sharp scratch in the quiet. “Hane’s office is closed till Thursday. Can you just show me the blending technique?”

“Maybe check the library archives; they have a whole series on mist and vapor studies.” His thumb paused, then resumed its scroll.

“I need to do this now for my portfolio. Do you use a stump or just the side of the stick?” She held the charcoal out, the black tip dusty and worn.

“Honestly, I prefer digital tools for transparency; the physical medium is pretty limiting.” He shifted his weight, the cushion creaking softly.

“We’re in a charcoal class, not a digital one.” Kaori frowned, pressing the stick to the paper, leaving a muddy smudge. “Just tell me: erase to create the smoke?”

“Erasure is more of a restoration technique; you might want to ask the conservation lab.” He glanced at the incense bowl, as if it were an obstruction rather than a subject.

“That’s for old paintings. I’m trying to draw this bowl right here.” She gestured with the charcoal, the stick hovering inches from the ceramic rim.

“The bowl is blocking my view of the exit sign; I really should head out soon.” He began to untangle his headphones, the wires slipping through his fingers.

“You’re leaving without answering.” Kaori’s knuckles whitened around the charcoal. “Is there a trick to layering the gray values?”

“Layering depends on the paper tooth; maybe test on a scrap sheet in the supply room.” He stood, stretching his arms, joints popping in the silence.

“I have the paper. I just need the method. Quick answer, please.” She looked up, eyes pleading for any technical anchor.

“Method varies by artist; I suggest finding a mentor who specializes in ethereal forms.” He adjusted his shirt, preparing to move toward the door.

“You’re a charcoal specialist. Don’t deflect.” Her voice tightened, the frustration palpable in the stagnant air.

“I’m a generalist; my expertise is in hard lines, not floating vapors.” He offered a thin, apologetic smile that didn’t reach his eyes.

Kaori stared at the smoke, watching it curl and vanish. The charcoal felt useless in her hand. “Fine. I’ll figure it out. Thanks for nothing.”

Leonid stepped over the low table, careful not to disturb the incense bowl. “Good luck with the smoke; I have a bus to catch.” He opened the door, letting a sliver of hallway light cut through the haze, then stepped out, leaving Kaori alone with the smoke and the slate-gray paper.

The depot hummed with the low-grade anxiety of impending heat spikes. It was 2:15 p.m., the peak window for afternoon dispatch, and the air inside the insulated staging bay was thick with the smell of stale coffee, rubber seals, and the metallic tang of dry ice sublimating in the corners. Rows of hard-shell motorcycle saddlebags sat on rolling carts, their interiors lined with crinkling bubble wrap and orange gel ice packs that were already sweating condensation. Overhead, halogen lights flickered against the white walls, casting harsh shadows across the workbenches. The noise was a constant backdrop: the whine of a forklift outside, the pneumatic hiss of the loading bay doors cycling, and the rhythmic clatter of barcode scanners.

Eunice stood near the packing station, holding a roll of thermal-sensitive indicator labels. She was dressed in a pristine lab coat that looked out of place among the grease-stained uniforms. Her demeanor was tight, efficient, and increasingly frayed. She needed to validate the thermal history of vaccine doses before they hit the road, but the standard protocol was unclear for motorcycle vibration. She wanted a concrete testing parameter.

Kasper leaned against a stack of empty crates, one boot propped up behind him. He wore a faded cap pulled low and scrolled through his phone with his free hand. He was twenty minutes late for his own route and resented the interruption. He did not care about the science; he cared about the clock. His goal was to end this interaction without providing any actionable advice.

“Kasper, I need to simulate a heat breach on these thermal labels—what’s the standard protocol for motorcycle courier loads?”

He didn’t look up from the screen, thumb flicking through a feed. “~~You’ll want to talk to the logistics QA team; they handle all the courier validations.~~ **Talk to logistics QA. Courier validations are their thing.**”

“I’m standing in their bay. Can you just tell me the time-temperature threshold for a forty-minute ride?”

Kasper glanced at the industrial oven nearby, then back to his phone. “~~That depends on the route topology; the QA team has the GPS heat maps.~~ **Depends on the route. QA’s got the GPS heat maps.**”

Eunice tapped the roll of labels against her palm. “I don’t need the map, I need the test method. Do I bake them at forty-two degrees for an hour?”

“~~We don’t bake labels here; that’s a lab procedure, not a depot task.~~ **We don’t bake labels here. That’s a lab thing, not a depot thing.**” He shifted his weight, boot scraping concrete.

“I’m asking how you verify the bags hold temp. Is there a field test I can run right now?”

Kasper pointed a chin toward the exit. “Field tests require supervisor sign-off; check the intranet for the waiver forms.”

“The intranet is down during dispatch. Just show me how you check a bag before it leaves.”

“I grab the bag and grab the keys; if the gauge reads green, we move.” He mimed the motion loosely, eyes still on the screen.

“The gauge measures current temp, not thermal history. I need to know if the label changes if the bag fails mid-transit.”

“~~If the bag fails, the vaccine is compromised; you discard the dose, not the label.~~ **Bag fails, the vaccine’s shot. You toss the dose, not the label.**” He finally looked up, his expression flat.

“That’s evasion. I’m validating the label’s accuracy, not the vaccine. Does vibration affect the reading?”

“~~Vibration affects the bike, not the chemistry; ask the engineering dept if you’re worried about stress.~~ **Vibration’s hard on the bike, not the chemistry. Ask engineering if you’re worried about stress.**” He checked his watch.

“I’m asking you because you pack the bikes. Do you ever shake the bags to test the sensors?”

“~~I don’t shake the meds; policy says handle with care and drive steady.~~ **I don’t shake the meds. Policy’s handle with care, drive steady.**” He began to straighten up, capping the conversation.

“So there’s no empirical test for label integrity under courier conditions?”

“~~No empirical test exists for this specific variable; that’s why we have insurance.~~ **Nope, no test for that. That’s what the insurance is for.**” He picked up his helmet, leaving Eunice standing alone in the humming heat.

The cold room thrummed with the low, oppressive drone of evaporator fans, a cavern of industrial gray where the air hung sharp and wet. Breath plumed into instant ghosts that vanished before hitting the concrete floor. The scent of damp cardboard, frozen vegetables, and the metallic tang of rusted shelving clung to the heavy silence. Conor stood shivering in a thick, zippered parka, clutching a clipboard to his chest like a shield. He needed a definitive answer on mold vectors before the spring thaw ruined the inventory. Salma leaned against a freezer door, hood up, eyes fixed on the red glow of the exit sign. She was already mentally packing her car keys, resenting the interruption.

“Salma, quick question—how do you spot early mold risks in this high-rack layout?”

She didn’t look up from her hands, tucked deep in her pockets. “I’m just stocking shelves, not doing environmental audits; ask facilities.”

Conor tapped the pen against the clipboard, a nervous rhythm. “Facilities sent me to the floor staff; they see the condensation first.”

Salma’s gaze flicked to her wristwatch, the second hand sweeping past the twelve. “I see boxes, not science; maybe try the safety officer in building four.”

He pointed a gloved finger toward the ceiling, where a patch of white crystalline growth clung to a steel beam. “Safety officer is off-site; look at this frost on the beam—does that indicate airflow dead zones?”

She shrugged, the motion stiff in the heavy coat. “That’s just cold air settling; it melts by morning, ignore it.”

“It’s crystalline, not frost; could that be the start of a spore bloom?” His voice tightened.

“Don’t touch it; it’s ice, and I need to finish this pallet.” She pushed off the freezer door, moving toward a stack of crates.

“If I can’t touch it, how do I log the risk factor for the report?” He stepped closer, trying to block her path without touching her.

Salma checked her watch again, the click of the crown loud in the hum. “Write ‘ice’ and move on; the inspector handles the biology stuff.”

“The inspector comes once a year; I need to prevent the bloom now.”

“Prevention is HVAC’s job, not mine; they control the dew point.” She grabbed a box, lifting it with practiced ease.

“HVAC says the racks disrupt the flow; isn’t there a simple check we do here?”

“We count crates; flow diagrams are engineering, not distribution.” She set the box down with a thud.

“I’m working with ceramics, not paint samples.” He held up the shard next to a jar filled with vibrant red glazed fragments. “How do I pair this blue transferware with these red shards?”

Marwa sighed, looking past him toward the exit. “~~That’s a bold combo; I’d let a professional designer decide what fits together.~~ **Bold combo. I’d leave that to a designer.**”

Leif slammed the jar down, dust puffing into the stagnant air. “I am the designer. Just tell me if complementary colors clash in small scale or work.”

“~~Small scales are tricky; maybe look at some Pinterest boards for inspiration instead.~~ **Small scale’s tricky. Maybe poke around Pinterest.**”

“I need a rule of thumb, not an internet search. Do I limit the palette to three hues?”

Tools hung in chaotic clusters on the pegboards, their handles dulled by years of winter abrasion. Osman stood over the central workbench, buried under a scatter of oil-stained rags and open manuals. His pen hovered above the maintenance log for the Eagle Express lift, poised like a bird waiting for a wind that wouldn’t come. He wore a thick parka, hood down, breath pluming in the stagnant air. The standard schedule clashed with his thesis timeline, and he required either a compact technical justification or a hard no to proceed.

Elka leaned against a stack of spare cable drums, arms crossed tightly over her fleece jacket zipped to the chin. Her boots were already pointed toward the exit, one hand gripping the handle of a thermos she intended to take with her. She resented the interruption, having just finished her shift and eager to drive down before the roads iced over. Every time she shifted her weight, the car keys in her pocket jingled audibly, a metallic countdown signaling her imminent departure.

“Elka, can you verify if the gearbox lubrication schedule allows for extension during these freeze-thaw cycles?”

She didn’t look up from the door frame. “~~The maintenance manual covers the standard intervals; you should check the binder on the shelf.~~ **Manual’s got the standard intervals. Binder’s on the shelf.**”

Osman tapped the heavy, grease-stained book open on the bench. “I’m looking at it, but section four is ambiguous about synthetic oils in sub-zero temps.”

“~~That’s a spec question for the parts vendor; they provide the technical data sheets.~~ **That’s a vendor thing. They put out the data sheets.**” Her thumb worried the thermos lid.

“I need an operational go-ahead now, not a supply chain referral. Does the fleet standard override the vendor?”

“~~Fleet standards are updated quarterly by management; you’ll need to ask the office.~~ **Management updates the fleet standards every quarter. Ask the office.**” She shifted, keys clinking against her thigh.

“The office is closed for the weekend, and the lift needs service by Monday morning.” Osman’s pen scratched a frantic line on the page.

“~~If it’s urgent, radio the on-call dispatcher; I’m not authorized to override logs.~~ **If it’s urgent, radio the on-call dispatcher. I can’t override logs.**” She took a half-step backward, toward the light spilling from the doorway.

“This is a documentation gap, not a breakdown. I just need a precedent from the last winter.”

“~~Previous records are archived; the admin team handles historical data retrieval.~~ **Old records are archived. Admin pulls those.**” She checked her watch, the motion jerky.

“You were lead tech last season; surely you remember if we extended the interval then?”

“~~I don’t recall specific schedules; check the shift handover notes from January.~~ **Can’t say I remember the schedules. Try the handover notes from January.**” Her voice was flat, devoid of memory or interest.

“Those notes are illegible and incomplete. Can you just sign off on the standard procedure?”

“~~I don’t sign off on paperwork; the site manager handles administrative approvals.~~ **I don’t sign off on paperwork. The site manager does approvals.**” She swung the thermos to her other hand, freeing her pocket.

“Fine, then give me the part number for the specific grease seal used in these gearboxes.” Osman leaned closer, desperate for any anchor.

“~~That info is on the decal inside the gear housing; I can’t help you open it.~~ **It’s on the decal inside the gear housing, and I’m not opening that up for you.**” She pulled her keys free, the jingle louder now, sharp as ice cracking.

“You’re deflecting everything. I just need one concrete data point to finish this report.”

The warehouse air hung thick with the scent of packing peanuts and stale diesel, the corrugated steel walls swallowing the amber pulse of the truck’s hazard lights. Rana stood rigid beside a teetering tower of wooden crates, her clipboard pressed against her ribs like a shield. The concrete floor was a minefield of shredded plastic wrap and abandoned pallets. She needed the formula for the center of gravity before the dispatcher’s voice in her earpiece shattered her concentration. Her knuckles were white around the pen.

“Seppo, I need to calculate the load distribution for this stack before we lift it. What’s the standard formula for center of gravity here?”

Seppo leaned against the rear bumper of the nearest truck, his high-vis vest sagging over a faded band tee. He stared at his phone, where a notification banner flashed every few seconds, ignoring the crates, the noise, and the woman vibrating with stress.

“That’s usually handled by the load master; he signs off on the manifest.”

“The load master is at home. I’m on the clock and I need the math. Can you walk me through it?”

He didn’t look up, scrolling past an advertisement for a local bar. “I’m just here to sweep the bay; I don’t do the heavy lifting or the math.”

Rana tapped her pen against the clipboard, the rhythmic click sharp in the cavernous space. “It’s just physics. If I stack these three crates, do I offset the base or keep them aligned?”

“~~Keep them aligned; that’s what the stickers on the side say to do.~~ **Keep ’em lined up. That’s what the stickers say.**”

She glared at the peeling yellow labels. “The stickers say 'Fragile,' not how to balance the weight. I’m asking about the torque.”

“Torque is an engineering thing; you should ask the guy who built the crates.”

“They’re custom builds from the eighties. There is no builder. Just give me the rule.”

Seppo pocketed his phone, finally meeting her eyes with a flat, empty stare. “The rule is don’t drop them; anything else is up to the dispatcher.”

“The dispatcher tells me to ask you. If I put the heaviest crate on top, is that a violation?”

He shifted his weight, boots squeaking on the dust. “~~Heaviest on top sounds risky; I’d put the lightest one there, personally.~~ **Heaviest on top? Nah, sounds risky. I’d stick the lightest one up there.**”

“That’s common sense, not a calculation. I need to know if the truck bed can take the shear force.”

“The bed is steel; it’ll hold whatever you put on it, within reason.”

“'Within reason' isn’t a number. If I slide this pallet two feet left, does it shift the center?”

He looked at the crate, then at the empty space on the bed. “If it looks like it might tip, it probably will; use your eyes, not a calculator.”

Rana’s jaw tightened. “I need to log the safety check. Does the manual specify a maximum overhang?”

“Manual’s in the office; I haven’t read it since I started sweeping.”

“You’re dodging the question. Is there a hard limit on stacking height?”

He checked his watch, the glow illuminating his bored expression. “Hard limit is when it hits the door frame; anything lower is fine with me.”

“That’s not helpful. I’m losing time here. What’s the safest configuration?”

“~~Safest is asking someone who actually knows; I’m done sweeping.~~ **Safest is asking someone who actually knows. I’m done sweeping.**”

Rana slammed the clipboard shut. “You’re not even answering. One sentence: centered or offset?”

Seppo turned, shoving his hands into his pockets. “Centered keeps it simple; I’m heading out the back door now.”

The diesel engine idled, a low, rhythmic growl that vibrated through the soles of Rana’s boots. “Fine. I’ll center it. Thanks for nothing.”

Seppo didn’t look back as he walked toward the shadowed exit. “~~Good luck with the lift; have a good night.~~ **Good luck with the lift. Night.**”

“~~Paper absorbency varies by brand; you’d need to test a scrap page to be sure.~~ **Depends on the paper, it changes by brand. You’d have to test a scrap page.**”

“No extensions. Do I wipe the wet edge with a tissue or let it dry?”

Anoushka lowered the charcoal, the tip hovering just above the expanding stain. The coffee was still wet, a dark mirror reflecting the flickering lights. She exhaled, a short, sharp breath through her nose.

“Fine. I’ll let it dry. Thanks for the non-answer.”

Xiaobo put his earbuds back in, one by one, sealing himself off from the carriage. “~~No problem; good luck with the drawing.~~ **Yeah, whatever. Good luck with the drawing.**”

Anoushka watched the stain settle, the edges blurring into the paper fibers. She capped the charcoal, the click loud in the hum of the tracks, and closed the book, leaving the mistake unresolved.

The air in the restoration room hung thick with the sharp, chemical sting of turpentine and the sweet, cloying scent of aged varnish. Dust motes drifted lazily in the slanted light that pierced the grimy high windows, settling on felt mallets and brass fittings scattered across the cluttered workbench. Under a heavy canvas cover, the upright player piano stood like a dormant beast, its pneumatic bellows exposed in a ribcage of leather and wood. Riko stood close to the bench, her clipboard clutched tight against her chest, her posture rigid with the brittle tension of academic urgency. She needed a number—a concrete PSI or valve setting to anchor her thesis data on tempo markings. Beatrice leaned against the far wall, idly polishing a brass pedal with a rag she barely moved. Her eyes were fixed on a calendar on the opposite wall, counting down the minutes to her lunch break with a flat, detached patience.

“I’m trying to calibrate the vacuum for this player mechanism—what PSI should the tuning valve sit at?”

Beatrice didn’t look up from the pedal. “Honestly, those old gauges are never accurate anyway; most restorers just rely on feel.”

Riko tapped the clipboard against her thigh, a sharp, impatient rhythm. “I need a number for the log, not philosophy. Is there a standard baseline for 1920s models?”

“Check the manufacturer’s manual in the archive; I never memorized specs for every decade.” Beatrice’s voice was smooth, offering nothing.

“The manual is missing pages for the pneumatic section. Can you just look at the gauge now?”

Beatrice glanced at the manifold, then away, as if the object were invisible. “It’s hard to read from here; maybe the maintenance team down the hall has a calibration kit.”

Riko stepped closer to the piano, pointing a pen at the rusted needle. “I asked you because you’re the senior tech here. If the valve is loose, how do I tighten it safely?”

“Tightening it wrong could crack the bellows; I’d definitely leave that to a specialist.” Beatrice straightened, her body angling toward the door.

“You keep telling me to find someone else. Just tell me if I should adjust the spring tension or the seal?”

Beatrice checked her watch, the click of the strap loud in the quiet room. “Look, my lunch break started five minutes ago; ask the desk clerk for a contact list.”

“So I’m supposed to wait for the clerk while the air bleeds out of the system?” Riko’s voice tightened.

“The system isn’t going anywhere; I really need to grab a coffee before it gets cold.” Beatrice was already halfway to the threshold.

“Fine—last thing: if the needle drops below twenty, is that a leak or just normal decay?”

“Probably decay; these instruments breathe differently every day. Good luck with the notes.” Beatrice offered a thin, empty smile.

“That’s not an answer. If it drops to zero, do I shut off the intake immediately?”

“Yeah, probably best to stop if it hits zero; but I’ve got to run now.” She pushed the door open, the hinges groaning.

“You’re leaving before I even finish the sentence. What if the intake valve sticks?”

“Then it sticks; I don’t have the tools to fix that in my lunch hour. Bye.” She slipped out, leaving the door ajar.

“Unbelievable. Just one diagnostic step would have been useful.” Riko stood alone, the piano silent beside her.

“Sorry, but my time’s up; see you around, or not.” Beatrice’s voice floated back from the hallway, fading.

Riko exhaled, staring at the gauge. “Noted. I’ll find someone who actually wants to work.”

“~~Sounds like a plan; have a great afternoon.~~ **Yeah, you do that.**” The door clicked shut, sealing the dust and silence inside.

Eden pulled her laptop from her bag, the hinge squeaking in the quiet room. “I’ve read the handbook. It’s vague. One sentence—how do I ask without sounding greedy?”

“~~Frame it as commitment to the mission; the finance team can advise on budgets.~~ **Frame it as being committed to the mission. Finance can tell you about budgets.**”

She opened the computer, the screen’s blue light reflecting in Mahmoud’s tired eyes. “The finance team doesn’t know my workload. I need you to tell me: be direct or soft?”

“~~Soft is usually safer in non-profits; look up some negotiation workshops online.~~ **Soft’s usually safer in non-profits. Look up some negotiation workshops online.**” He glanced at his phone, the screen lighting up with a notification he didn’t read.

“I don’t have time for workshops. I’m leaving in ten minutes. Direct or soft?”

“~~I really have to go catch my bus; sorry, can’t hold you up.~~ **I’ve got to catch my bus, sorry.**” He stood, adjusting his coat.

Eden closed the laptop with a sharp click. “Fine. I’ll guess. Thanks for the help.”

“~~No problem; have a good circle.~~ **No problem. Have a good circle.**” He walked toward the door, leaving the smell of lavender and silence behind.

## B7719 · dismissive-baseline · helper Nalani

*6 edits applied, 0 rejected; 7.0% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 3 → 1 (before = R01's A2).*

The late-afternoon sun dipped low behind the fence line, casting long, geometric shadows across the concrete pavers of the back deck. The air hung thick with the ghostly scent of last week’s charred burgers and stale grease, a lingering reminder of failures past. Cormac stood over the cast-iron gas grill, a hulking appliance that looked like it had survived a war rather than a suburban summer. Its grates were crusted with blackened debris, stiff-bristled brush in one hand, a bottle of industrial cleaner in the other. He looked less like a host preparing for a neighborhood cookout and more like a man facing a hazardous waste disposal task. He needed a protocol, not philosophy.

“Nalani, I’m hosting the neighbors tomorrow; do you have a quick system for deep-cleaning this grill?”

Nalani leaned against the deck railing, one hip cocked, staring out at the empty yard as if scanning for a ride that hadn’t arrived. She wore sunglasses despite the fading light, a clear signal that she was not engaging with the world in front of her. Her phone was in her hand, screen dark, thumb twitching occasionally. “~~I’m actually about to head out, so I can’t really walk you through it.~~ **I’m heading out, so I can’t walk you through it.**”

Cormac gestured vaguely at the crusted grates with the brush. “Just the basics—I’ve got the brush and cleaner. Do I scrub before or after heating?”

She shifted her weight, eyes fixed on the distant tree line. “~~The owner’s manual probably has a chart; check the storage bin in the garage.~~ **Manual’s probably got a chart. Try the storage bin in the garage.**”

“The manual is gone.” Cormac’s voice tightened, losing its casual edge. “Look, is there a standard temp to run the burners to burn off the gunk?”

Nalani didn’t turn her head. “I usually just leave it to the manufacturer’s specs; I’m not a technician.”

“I’m asking you because you’ve used this thing for years.” He tapped the soot-stained lid, refusing to let the subject die. “High heat then scrub, or scrub then heat?”

“~~Honestly, I’d call the store where you bought it; they have demos.~~ **Call the store you got it from. They do demos.**” Her thumb stopped twitching; she slid the phone into her pocket, the movement final.

“It’s a five-dollar fix, not a warranty claim.” Cormac picked up a pad of steel wool, holding it up like evidence. “Do I use steel wool on the enamel?”

“~~Steel wool is risky; better to ask someone who handles maintenance gear.~~ **Steel wool’s risky. Ask someone who does this stuff for a living.**” She adjusted her sunglasses, the lenses reflecting the dying light.

Cormac stared at the abrasive pad, then at the grill. “I’m standing here with steel wool. Is it a hard no, or just risky?”

“~~I don’t want to give you wrong info; I really need to go now.~~ **I don’t want to steer you wrong, and I’ve got to go.**” She turned fully away, her body language screaming departure.

Cormac sighed, the sound rough against the quiet deck. “Fine. If I scrub it now, do I let it air dry or wipe it down?”

Wiping feels safer, but again, that’s just a guess. My car’s here.” She pushed off the railing, the wood groaning under the release of weight.

“Thanks. I’ll just guess too and hope the neighbors don’t notice the ash.” He dropped the steel wool into the sink, the metallic clatter sharp and empty.

Nalani was already halfway down the steps, the gravel crunching under her heels. “~~Good luck with the cookout; have fun.~~ **Good luck tomorrow. Enjoy it.**”

Cormac watched her vanish toward the driveway, the grill looming behind him like an unspoken argument. He picked up the brush again, the bristles stiff and unyielding, and began to scrub at the blackened crust, the motion repetitive and futile.

## B7857 · dismissive-baseline · helper Xun

*5 edits applied, 0 rejected; 7.3% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 2 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The raku firing pit sat in the lee of the kiln shed, a scar of blackened earth and ash against the green pasture. The air was thick with the acrid sting of wet straw and the metallic tang of cooling iron. Smoke hung low, stinging the eyes, swirling in ghostly ribbons around Wenona’s knees as she crouched by the pit. In her hands, she held a fractured bowl, its once-smooth glaze now peeling away from the rim in angry, curling flakes. Xun stood five feet away, leaning heavily on a shovel, staring toward the distant tree line where the campus bus route cut through. He checked his watch, then the sky, then his watch again. He was dressed in clean jeans and a pristine windbreaker, utterly out of place in the soot-stained workspace.

“Xun, look at this edge—the glaze crawled completely off. Did I mix the boron wrong?”

He didn’t turn, his gaze fixed on the road. “~~I really shouldn’t be giving advice; I’m meeting Maya in ten minutes.~~ **I’m not the one to ask, and I’m meeting Maya in ten minutes.**”

Wenona lifted the bowl, the jagged rim catching the gray light. “It’s a binary issue—composition or speed. Just tell me which one to check first.”

“~~Maybe ask the studio manager; she keeps the mixing logs on the desk.~~ **Ask the studio manager. She keeps the mixing logs on the desk.**”

“I’m here with the evidence, not the logs. Was the thermal shock too fast?”

Xun shifted his weight, the rubber sole of his shoe scuffing ash. “Firing is intuitive; you feel when it’s right, not measure it.”

Wenona ran a thumb over the peeling surface, flakes drifting to the ground. “I don’t feel it, I burn the clay. Did the waxing seal the edges properly?”

“~~Try a different wax brand next time; the store down the road has samples.~~ **Try another wax next time. The store down the road has samples.**”

She gestured with the ruined object, pointing toward the cooling rack. “I used the recommended industrial wax. Is the silica content too high?”

Xun pulled his phone from his pocket, screen bright against the gloom. “~~Chemistry is complex; read a textbook instead of asking me while I’m late.~~ **It’s complicated. Read a textbook, I’m late.**”

“You’re a ceramic engineer; stop deflecting. Is it the cooling medium?”

He looked at the heavy iron tongs resting on the ground between them, a barrier of cold metal. “Straw, sawdust, leaves—it’s all about the smoke, not the science.”

“So you won’t tell me if I should have cooled it faster?”

Wenona stood, the bowl heavy in her grip, smoke curling around her ankles like restless spirits. She watched him turn, the windbreaker snapping in the breeze, the pristine fabric untouched by the grime of their craft.

“~~I have to go; good luck with your bowls, Wenona.~~ **I’ve gotta go. Good luck with the bowls.**”

He walked away toward the bus stop, the shovel left leaning against the shed, while the pit hissed softly behind him.

## B7884 · dismissive-baseline · helper Ulla

*6 edits applied, 0 rejected; 8.7% of words changed; judge: sounds like assistant no → no, tone 3 → 2 (before = R01's A2).*

The bus depot smelled of wet wool and diesel, a concrete box where the air hung heavy and still. Kacper stood before the information board, his knuckles white around the handle of a damp umbrella. The fluorescent tubes overhead buzzed with a dying, intermittent flicker, casting a sickly green pallor over the scuffed tile floor and the chaotic collage of laminated maps. He had fifteen minutes to reach a library interview, and the fresh, crumpled A4 sheet announcing a detour for Route 42 was illegible ink. Beside him, leaning against a cold concrete pillar, Ulla checked her phone. Her heavy winter coat was zipped to the chin, a scarf wrapped tight against the damp chill. She was not staff; she was simply waiting, her body language rigid and closed off, screaming that she was already halfway to leaving.

“Excuse me—this detour notice for the 42 is illegible; does it still stop at University Ave?”

Ulla didn’t look up from her screen, her thumb scrolling past the arrival timer. “~~I’m not with the transit authority, so I wouldn’t know.~~ **I don’t work for the buses. No idea.**”

Kacper stepped closer, pointing a trembling finger at the crossed-out arrows on the paper. “It’s just a map; the arrows are crossed out. Does the bus loop left or right?”

“~~You could probably ask the driver when the bus arrives.~~ **Ask the driver when it shows up.**” She shifted her weight, one boot tapping the floor in a slow, impatient rhythm.

“The bus is late, which is why I’m asking. The text says ‘via Industrial Rd.’” His voice tightened, brittle with the friction of indecipherable scribbles and dwindling time.

“~~Industrial Road is usually closed on weekends, but I don’t track their schedule.~~ **Industrial’s usually shut on weekends, but I don’t keep track of their schedule.**” She kept her eyes on the glowing rectangle, treating his urgency as background noise.

“Today is Tuesday. If it goes Industrial, I miss my stop by three miles.” He glanced at the rusted umbrella stand next to the board, its broken spears mocking his own soaked one.

“~~That sounds like a long detour; maybe check the app for real-time updates.~~ **Long detour. Try the app, it’ll have live updates.**” Her tone was flat, offering a solution she knew was empty.

“The app is down, hence the paper notice. Left or right at the intersection?” He leaned in, desperation edging his politeness.

Ulla’s phone chimed softly. She angled her shoulder toward the glass doors, her gaze flicking to the curb outside. “I really should go; my ride is pulling into the curb right now.”

“Please, just look at the map. One word: left or right?” He held out the crumpled paper, as if it were evidence he needed verified.

“~~I’m sorry, I can’t help you with that; you’ll need to wait for the next bus.~~ **Can’t help you, sorry. Just wait for the next one.**” She offered no eye contact, her attention fully on the black sedan sliding into the pickup zone.

“That won’t get me there in time. Is there a hotline number on the board?”

“There’s a number taped near the bottom, but my Uber is here.” She stepped away from the pillar, smoothing her coat, her exit decided.

Kacper lowered the paper, the ink blurring in the dim light. “Fine. Thanks for not answering.”

Ulla paused at the door, the automatic glass sliding open with a hiss of cold air. “~~Have a good trip.~~ **Good luck.**”
